\subsection{The second order at the root system}\label{sec:second-order}

Statement (ii) of the list above asks for the volume of the cell of
twenty-four centres near the root system beyond the explicit neighbourhood of
\cref{thm:near-contact}. Its step (b) charges the tilts of the directions a
loss of order $10^2S^2$, from $73S^2$ at $S=10^{-3}$ to $98S^2$ at
$S=4\cdot10^{-3}$, against the gain
$\frac23S$, and that stops it. This subsection computes the second
order exactly, certifies its minimum on the packing cone, integrates the
push-outs exactly, and says at its end what separates the result from
statement (ii).

\begin{lem}[Second variation of the volume of a $4$-polytope]\label{lem:second-variation}
Let $P(t)=\{x:\langle x,n_i(t)\rangle\le h_i(t)\}$ be a bounded polytope of
$\mathbb R^4$ with unit normals $n_i(t)$ moving along geodesics of $S^3$,
$\dot n_i(0)=\tau_i\perp n_i(0)$, and $h_i(t)=h_i+t\eta_i$. Write
$v_i(x)=\eta_i-\langle x,\tau_i\rangle$ for the normal velocity of facet $i$
at $t=0$, $F_i$ for the facets, $F_{ij}=F_i\cap F_j$ for the $2$-faces, and
$\theta_{ij}$ for the angle between $n_i$ and $n_j$ on a $2$-face. Then
$t\mapsto\vol P(t)$ is twice continuously differentiable, with
\begin{align*}
  \frac{d}{dt}\vol P\Big|_{0}&=\sum_i\int_{F_i}v_i\,dA,\\
  \frac{d^2}{dt^2}\vol P\Big|_{0}&=\sum_i h_i|\tau_i|^2\,\vol_3F_i
  +\sum_{i<j}\frac1{\sin\theta_{ij}}\int_{F_{ij}}
  \bigl(2v_iv_j-\cos\theta_{ij}\,(v_i^2+v_j^2)\bigr)\,dA_2 .
\end{align*}
\end{lem}

\begin{proof}
The first formula is the transport theorem. For the second, follow each
facet by the purely normal motion $\dot x=v_in_i$, which keeps $x$ on the
moving hyperplane and, that hyperplane being flat, preserves the area
element to first order; along it $Dv_i/Dt=\ddot h_i-\langle x,\ddot n_i\rangle
=|\tau_i|^2\langle x,n_i\rangle=|\tau_i|^2h_i$ on $F_i$, since $\ddot n_i=-|\tau_i|^2n_i$
on a geodesic. The $2$-face $F_{ij}$ moves, relative to the material of
facet $i$ and along the conormal $\nu_i$ of $F_i$ at that face, at speed
$(v_j-v_i\cos\theta_{ij})/\sin\theta_{ij}$, because $n_j=\cos\theta_{ij}\,n_i+\sin\theta_{ij}\,\nu_i$
there; the transport theorem on the moving facet then gives
$\frac{d}{dt}\int_{F_i}v_i=\int_{F_i}|\tau_i|^2h_i+\sum_{j}\int_{F_{ij}}v_i(v_j-v_i\cos\theta_{ij})/\sin\theta_{ij}$,
and adding the two contributions of each $2$-face gives the formula. A
$2$-face that appears or disappears under the motion does so with area
tending to $0$, so both formulas are continuous across combinatorial changes;
the second derivative is therefore continuous, and $\vol P$ is $C^2$.\footnote{In
  dimension $n$ the $k$-th variation is an integral over the $(n-k)$-faces and
  the first jumps sit at the vertices, in the $n$-th derivative: the area of a
  polygon is only $C^1$ in its edge data, and the volume of a polytope of
  $\mathbb R^4$ is $C^3$.}
\end{proof}

\begin{prop}[The Hessian at the root system]\label{prop:hessian-d4}
At $Q=\{x:\langle x,u_i\rangle\le1\}$, in the coordinates
$\xi=(\tau,\eta)\in\mathbb R^{72}\times\mathbb R^{24}$, $\tau_i\perp u_i$, with
$\delta_i=2\eta_i$ the push-out of the $i$-th centre, the volume of $V(Y)$ has
the expansion
\[
  \vol V(Y)\;=\;8+\tfrac23\sum_i\delta_i+\tfrac12\,H(\xi,\xi)+O(|\xi|^3),
\]
where $H$ is the form of \cref{lem:second-variation} with $\vol_3F_i=\tfrac43$,
$\theta_{ij}=60^\circ$ and $F_{ij}$ the $96$ equilateral triangles of side
$\sqrt2$; by the midpoint rule, exact for quadratics,
\[
  H(\xi,\xi)=\tfrac43\sum_i|\tau_i|^2
  +\sum_{\{i,j\}\in T}\ \frac{1}{3}\sum_{m}
  \bigl(2v_i(m)v_j(m)-\tfrac12(v_i(m)^2+v_j(m)^2)\bigr),
\]
$m$ running over the midpoints of the edges of $F_{ij}$. The form is
indefinite: in orthonormal coordinates its least eigenvalue is $-8$, its
largest $4$, and its kernel has dimension $15$, the six rotations and nine
further flat directions. On the push-outs alone ($\tau=0$) it is
$H(\eta,\eta)=2\sum_{T}\eta_i\eta_j-4\sum_i\eta_i^2$, with matrix
$\mathrm{Adj}-4I$ for the edge graph of the $24$-cell, and
\begin{equation}\label{eq:eta-identity}
  H(\eta,\eta)+\Bigl(\sum_i\delta_i\Bigr)^2=2\sum_{T}\eta_i\eta_j+8\sum_{i<j}\eta_i\eta_j\ \ge\ 0
  \qquad(\eta\ge0),
\end{equation}
with equality exactly when at most one $\eta_i$ is nonzero.
\end{prop}

\begin{proof}
The expansion is Taylor's formula for the $C^2$ function of
\cref{lem:second-variation}; the first derivative is $\frac43\sum_i\eta_i$
because the centroid of each facet lies on its normal. The data are those of
the regular $24$-cell. With $\tau=0$ each triangle contributes
$2\eta_i\eta_j-\frac12(\eta_i^2+\eta_j^2)$, and each vertex of the edge graph
lies on eight edges, which gives $H(\eta,\eta)$; adding
$(\sum_i\delta_i)^2=4\sum_i\eta_i^2+8\sum_{i<j}\eta_i\eta_j$ gives
\eqref{eq:eta-identity}. The spectrum is computed in floating point, and
the form is checked against second differences of the exact volume of the
polytope, computed afresh by intersecting halfspaces, to $6\cdot10^{-8}$.
\end{proof}

\begin{prop}[The worst second-order loss on the packing cone]\label{prop:cone-min}
Let $\mathcal C$ be the first-order feasible cone of the packing constraints,
$(\Lambda\tau)_{ij}\le\tfrac12(\eta_i+\eta_j)$ on the $96$ tight pairs and
$\eta_i\ge0$. Then
\[
  m\;:=\;\min\bigl\{H(\xi,\xi):\ \xi\in\mathcal C,\ \textstyle\sum_i\delta_i=1\bigr\}\;=\;-1 ,
\]
attained on the ray of a single centre pushed out, where
$\vol=\tfrac{25}3-\tfrac13(1-\tfrac\delta2)^4=8+\tfrac23\delta-\tfrac12\delta^2+\tfrac16\delta^3-\tfrac1{48}\delta^4$
exactly. Consequently, for packings whose tilts are of order $S$, as estimate
(a) of the proof of \cref{thm:near-contact} makes them when $d(W)\le\frac1{48}$,
the second-order model of the volume is
\[
  \vol V(Y)-8\ \ge\ \tfrac23S-\tfrac12S^2+O(S^3),\qquad S=\sum_i\delta_i ,
\]
positive up to $S=\tfrac43$, against the $0.155$ at which the pair terms of
\cref{lem:no-triple} take over.
\end{prop}

\begin{proof}
$m\le-1$ is the ray. For $m\ge-1$ it suffices that $H+cc^{\mathsf T}$,
$c=2\mathbf 1_\eta$, be copositive on $\mathcal C=\{\xi:B\xi\ge0\}$, $B$ the
$120$ rows of the cone, since $c\cdot\xi=\sum_i\delta_i$; and a certificate is
\[
  H+cc^{\mathsf T}\;=\;P+B^{\mathsf T}NB,\qquad P\succeq0,\quad N\ge0\ \text{entrywise},
\]
since then $\xi^{\mathsf T}(H+cc^{\mathsf T})\xi=\xi^{\mathsf T}P\xi+(B\xi)^{\mathsf T}N(B\xi)\ge0$
on $\mathcal C$. Such a pair exists, and it is verified with no floating
point:
in the scaled integral model of $Q$ (roots $(\pm1,\pm1,0,0)$, vertices
$(\pm2,0,0,0)$ and $(\pm1,\pm1,\pm1,\pm1)$, rational bases of the tangent
spaces, $t_i=\sqrt2\,\tau_i$) the form $H$, the rows $B$ and $c$ are
rational; $N$ is symmetric, constant on the $43$ orbits of the
signed-permutation group on unordered pairs of constraints, with
nonnegative rational values, $35$ of them positive; and $P$ is assembled
exactly and is positive semidefinite by an exact $LDL^{\mathsf T}$, whose $30$
zero pivots are the push-outs and the rotations. The certificate was found as
the dual of the Shor relaxation with the products of the cone constraints,
whose value is $-1$, averaged over the group and made
exact. That the rows $B$ are the first-order packing constraints, the
derivative of $|y_i-y_j|^2$ being $8(B\xi)_{ij}$ on the tight pairs, is checked
against the exact distances to $2\cdot10^{-9}$.
The verification is repeated in Lean, where $H$,
$B$ and $c$ are built from the root system in the proof assistant itself
(\cref{sec:reproducibility-new}).
\end{proof}

\begin{figure}[tb]
\centering
\includegraphics[width=\textwidth]{fig_tikz_secondorder}
\caption{The second order at the root system.
(a) $\vol V(Y)-8$ against $S=\sum_i\delta_i$: the second-order model
$\frac23S-\frac12S^2$, which is the worst case on the packing cone
(\cref{prop:cone-min}), the exact value with one centre pushed out, on which
it is sharp to second order, and the exact value with all twenty-four pushed
out evenly; the dotted line is where the pair terms of \cref{lem:no-triple}
reach $8$ along the even push. Step (b) of the proof of
\cref{thm:near-contact} ends at $S=4.5\cdot10^{-3}$. (b) What is left over,
$\vol V(Y)-8-\frac23S+\frac12S^2$, on $300$ packings near the root system with
tilts along the edges of the cone of up to $5.8\,S$
(computed in floating point), and
exactly on the one-centre ray, $\frac16\delta^3-\frac1{48}\delta^4$: it is
positive on every one, and least on that ray.}
\label{fig:second-order}
\end{figure}

\begin{remark}[What the second order gives for (ii), and what it does not]\label{rem:second-order-gap}
The expansion says that the second-order loss on the cone is exactly the
loss on the deleted root's axis, $\tfrac12S^2$: the tilts, which step (b) of
\cref{thm:near-contact} charges at order $10^2S^2$, cost nothing more at
second order than the pushes do. Three things separate this from statement
(ii). Tilting is not monotone: there are feasible packings
near the root system whose cell is smaller than that of the same distances
at the roots, in $103$ of $237$ trials and by up to $3.6\cdot10^{-5}$, and by
$0.117\,S^2$ at second order in the worst push pattern, so the bound does not
reduce to monotonicity in the support numbers. The remainder is not bounded:
on the $300$ packings of \cref{fig:second-order}(b), with $S$ up to $0.48$,
the exact volume satisfies
\[
  \vol V(Y)-8-\tfrac23S+\tfrac12S^2\ \ge\ 0
\]
in every case, with the minimum on the one-centre family, and the third-order
remainder $\vol-8-\tfrac23S-\tfrac12H(\xi,\xi)$ lies between $-0.004\,S^3$ and
$+0.166\,S^3$; the statement that carries (ii) to the pair terms, and well
past them, is that the displayed inequality holds for every packing with
$d(W)\le\tfrac1{48}$, and a proof through a bound on the remainder has to
control the cubic term to the size it has on the tested packings,
$0.004\,S^3$, where the estimates of the proof of \cref{thm:near-contact}
have constants of order $10^2$ already at second order. The natural
structure of such a proof is that the extremal family is the one-centre
ray, on which everything is explicit. And the inequality concerns $V(Y)$,
while far centres may cut off the part of $V(Y)$ outside the inversion hull
$K(Y)$ (\cref{prop:inversion-hull}), which part (c) of the proof of
\cref{thm:near-contact} bounds by $13\,958\,\Theta^4$. That bound is far
from the truth: along the ray of one centre pushed out and along the even
push, $\vol(V(Y)\setminus K(Y))$ stays below $10^{-6}$ up to $S=0.197$,
where the bound is $21$, and it grows as the seventh power of $S$ on the
first ray and as the fourth on the second.
Statement (ii) needs a hull estimate of that quality beside the inequality
above.
\end{remark}

\begin{lem}[Pure push-outs, exactly]\label{lem:pure-push}
For $0\le\eta_i\le1$,
\[
  \vol\{x:\langle x,u_i\rangle\le1+\eta_i\ \forall i\}\ \ge\ 8+\frac13\sum_i\bigl(1-(1-\eta_i)^4\bigr),
\]
with equality whenever at most one $\eta_i$ is nonzero. In particular, with
$S=2\sum_i\eta_i$, the right side is at least $8+\tfrac23S-\tfrac12S^2$.
\end{lem}

\begin{proof}
Along $h(t)=\mathbf 1+t\eta$ the volume has derivative $\sum_i\eta_iA_i(t)$,
$A_i(t)$ the $3$-volume of the $i$-th facet. That facet is
$\{x:\langle x,u_i\rangle=1+t\eta_i,\ \langle x,u_j\rangle\le1+t\eta_j\ (j\ne i)\}$,
which contains the facet with the other support numbers lowered to $1$, so
$A_i(t)\ge\tfrac43(1-t\eta_i)^3$ by the one-centre formula
$\vol=\tfrac{25}3-\tfrac13(1-s)^4$ at $h=\mathbf 1+se_i$. Integrating,
$\vol-8\ge\sum_i\eta_i\int_0^1\tfrac43(1-t\eta_i)^3dt=\tfrac13\sum_i(1-(1-\eta_i)^4)$.
For the last claim, $1-(1-\eta)^4=4\eta-6\eta^2+\eta^3(4-\eta)$ and
$\sum\eta_i^2\le(\sum\eta_i)^2$.
\end{proof}

\begin{prop}[The push-outs integrated out]\label{prop:push-integrated}
Let $w_1,\dots,w_{24}$ be unit vectors, $Q_w=\{x:\langle x,w_j\rangle\le1\ \forall j\}$
their contact cell, $A_i^w$ the $3$-volume of its $i$-th facet, and
$s_i=\max\{\langle x,w_i\rangle:\langle x,w_j\rangle\le1\ \forall j\ne i\}-1$ the
height of the apex of the $i$-th deletion above that facet. Then for
$0\le\eta_i\le s_i$,
\[
  \vol\{x:\langle x,w_i\rangle\le1+\eta_i\ \forall i\}\ \ge\
  \vol(Q_w)+\sum_i A_i^w\,\frac{s_i}{4}\Bigl(1-\bigl(1-\tfrac{\eta_i}{s_i}\bigr)^4\Bigr).
\]
\end{prop}

\begin{proof}
As in \cref{lem:pure-push}, the derivative along $\mathbf 1+t\eta$ is
$\sum_i\eta_iA_i(t)$ and $A_i(t)\ge a_i(t\eta_i)$, where $a_i(s)$ is the
$3$-volume of the section of the deletion $\{\langle x,w_j\rangle\le1,\ j\ne i\}$
by the hyperplane $\langle x,w_i\rangle=1+s$. The deletion is convex, so by
the Brunn--Minkowski inequality $a_i(s)^{1/3}$ is concave on $[0,s_i]$, and
it is nonnegative at $s_i$; hence $a_i(s)\ge(1-s/s_i)^3a_i(0)$ with
$a_i(0)=A_i^w$. Integrate.
\end{proof}

At the root system $s_i=1$ and $A_i^w=\tfrac43$, and
\cref{prop:push-integrated} is \cref{lem:pure-push}. In general it removes
the push-outs from the problem: the inequality of \cref{rem:second-order-gap}
reduces to one involving only the three functionals
$\vol(Q_w)$, $A_i^w$ and $s_i$ of the tilted directions, and the packing
constraints $\langle w_i,w_j\rangle\le\tfrac12+\tfrac12(\eta_i+\eta_j)+O(\eta^2)$
that tie the tilts to the pushes. At second order that is
\cref{prop:cone-min}; beyond it, what statement (ii) asks for is bounds on
the three functionals to second order in the tilts with explicit
remainders. The difficulty lies in the tilts, not the
pushes.\footnote{On $63$ tilted packings the least excess of the volume
  over the bound of \cref{prop:push-integrated} is $3.9\cdot10^{-6}$;
  \cref{lem:pure-push} holds on $400$ random push patterns, and the
  one-centre formula agrees with the computed volume to $10^{-14}$.}

Setting the push-outs to zero isolates the tilts. The block of $H$ on the
$72$ tangent coordinates is the Hessian of $\vol(Q_w)$, the volume of the
contact cell, as a function of the directions alone, with no packing
constraint.

\begin{prop}[The tilt block]\label{prop:tilt-block}
The restriction of the form $H$ of \cref{prop:hessian-d4} to pure tilts,
$\eta=0$, is positive semidefinite. Its kernel has dimension $15$: the six
infinitesimal rotations and nine further directions, none of them an
infinitesimal strain $\tau_i=Su_i-\langle u_i,Su_i\rangle u_i$ with $S$
symmetric.
\end{prop}

\begin{proof}
An exact $LDL^{\mathsf T}$ factorisation of the $72\times72$ block over $\mathbb{Q}$
has $57$ positive pivots, the least $\tfrac5{66}$, and $15$ zero pivots, each
with the rest of its row zero; the kernel is computed exactly, contains the
six rotation fields, and meets the nine strain fields only in $0$. The
factorisation is repeated in Lean.
\end{proof}

Thus the root system is a critical point of $\vol(Q_w)$ at which no tilt
lowers the volume at second order. By \cref{prop:push-integrated}, a lower
bound $\vol(Q_w)\ge8$ on an explicit neighbourhood of the root system would
give the first inequality of statement (ii) for every $Y$ whose directions
lie in it, with no packing constraint and no rigidity estimate, leaving the
hull bound and statement (i) to place the directions there. The
neighbourhood cannot be the whole space of direction sets. Without the
packing constraint the contact cell can be smaller than the $24$-cell: a set
of $24$ unit directions with inner products up to $0.579$ has a contact
cell with $24$ facets, $99$ vertices and volume $7.96553$, the same to eight
digits whether computed directly or as a quarter of its surface area.

The lower bound, if true, is not strict. The kernel in
\cref{prop:tilt-block} contains the tangent of a closed curve of direction
sets, none of them a rotation of the root system except its endpoints, along
which the contact cell keeps volume exactly $8$.

\begin{prop}[A closed curve of contact cells of volume $8$]\label{prop:hexagon-loop}
Write $\mathbb{R}^4=P\oplus P'$, with $P$ the plane of an $A_2$ hexagon of
roots, and $e(a)=(\cos a,\sin a)$ in orthonormal coordinates of either
plane. Up to a rotation, the $24$ unit roots are $(e(60^\circ m),0)$,
$m=0,\dots,5$, and the eighteen vectors
$\bigl(\sigma e(a),\sqrt{1-\sigma^2}\,e(b)\bigr)$ with $\sigma=1/\sqrt3$ and
$(a,b)$ in $\{30^\circ,150^\circ,270^\circ\}\times\{0^\circ,120^\circ,240^\circ\}$
or in $\{90^\circ,210^\circ,330^\circ\}\times\{60^\circ,180^\circ,300^\circ\}$.
For $0\le\theta\le\pi/3$ let $w(\theta)$ be the same list with the first six
turned to $(e(\theta+60^\circ m),0)$ and $\sigma$ replaced by
\[
  \sigma(\theta)=\frac{4C}{4C^2+3},\qquad C=\cos\bigl(\tfrac\pi6-\theta\bigr).
\]
Then $\vol Q_{w(\theta)}=8$ for every $\theta$. The sets $w(0)$ and
$w(\pi/3)$ are the root system; for $0<\theta<\pi/3$ the largest inner
product in $w(\theta)$ is at least $4C^2/(4C^2+3)>\frac12$. Therefore $w(\theta)$ is not a rotation of the root system,
and it violates the packing constraint. For fixed $\theta$ and any other
$\sigma\in(0,\frac{\sqrt3}2)$ the contact cell has volume above $8$.
\end{prop}

\begin{proof}
Write $x=(p,q)$. The first six constraints say that $p$ lies in the regular
hexagon $\mathrm{Hex}_\theta$ of inradius $1$ with edge normals
$e(\theta+60^\circ m)$. For such $p$ the other eighteen say
$\langle q,e(b)\rangle\le h_+$ for $b\in\{0^\circ,120^\circ,240^\circ\}$ and
$\langle q,e(b)\rangle\le h_-$ for $b\in\{60^\circ,180^\circ,300^\circ\}$, with
$h_\pm=(1-\sigma m_\pm(p))/\sqrt{1-\sigma^2}$, where $m_+$ and $m_-$ are the
support functions of the triangles $\{e(30^\circ),e(150^\circ),e(270^\circ)\}$
and $\{e(90^\circ),e(210^\circ),e(330^\circ)\}$. Since $m_\pm\le|p|\le2/\sqrt3$
and $\sigma<\sqrt3/2$, both are positive. The slice over $p$ is the
intersection of two opposite equilateral triangles of inradii $h_+$ and
$h_-$. If $h_+\le2h_-$ and $h_-\le2h_+$ it is a hexagon of area
$\sqrt3\,(4h_+h_--h_+^2-h_-^2)$; otherwise it is the smaller triangle, whose area
exceeds that expression by $\sqrt3\,(h_+-2h_-)^2$ or $\sqrt3\,(h_--2h_+)^2$.
Integrating over $\mathrm{Hex}_\theta$, of area $2\sqrt3$,
\[
  (1-\sigma^2)\vol Q\ \ge\ 12-2\sqrt3\,\sigma J_1+\sqrt3\,\sigma^2J_2,
\]
where $J_1=\int(m_++m_-)$ and $J_2=\int(4m_+m_--m_+^2-m_-^2)$ over
$\mathrm{Hex}_\theta$, with equality when no slice is a triangle. Rotation by $60^\circ$ maps
$\mathrm{Hex}_\theta$ to itself and exchanges $m_+$ and $m_-$, so $J_1$
and $J_2$ are six times their integrals over the sector between the rays at
$30^\circ$ and $90^\circ$. There $m_+=\langle p,e(30^\circ)\rangle$ and
$m_-=\langle p,e(90^\circ)\rangle$ are linear, and the sector of
$\mathrm{Hex}_\theta$ is the union of the triangles $0,\,e(30^\circ)/C,\,v$ and
$0,\,v,\,e(90^\circ)/C$, where $v=\frac{2}{\sqrt3}e(\theta+30^\circ)$ is the vertex
of $\mathrm{Hex}_\theta$ in it. Integrating over the two triangles gives
\[
  J_1=\frac{\sqrt3\,(4C^2+3)}{3C},\qquad J_2=\frac{\sqrt3\,(16C^4-8C^2+9)}{12\,C^2},
\]
and then
$4C^2\bigl(12-2\sqrt3\,\sigma J_1+\sqrt3\,\sigma^2J_2-8(1-\sigma^2)\bigr)
=\bigl(4C-\sigma(4C^2+3)\bigr)^2$. Hence $\vol Q\ge8$, with equality exactly
when $\sigma=\sigma(\theta)$ and no slice is a triangle. At $\sigma(\theta)$ the
conditions $h_\pm\le2h_\mp$ are linear in $p$ on each triangle. At its
vertices they reduce to $4C^2\ge3$ and to
$3\mp2\sqrt3\sin2\varphi\ge0$ with $\varphi=\frac\pi6-\theta$, or hold
trivially, and both hold for $|\varphi|\le\frac\pi6$. The inner product of
$(e(\theta),0)$ with $(\sigma e(30^\circ),\cdot)$ is
$\sigma C=4C^2/(4C^2+3)$, which exceeds $\frac12$ exactly when $4C^2>3$. At
$\theta=0$ the Gram matrix is that of the unit roots under an explicit
labelling, and $w(\pi/3)=w(0)$ as a set. The identities, and the Gram
matrix at $\theta=0$, are checked symbolically and again in Lean; as an
independent check, $|\vol Q_{w(\theta)}-8|$ is below $10^{-14}$ at thirteen
points in double precision and below $10^{-48}$ at three with $50$-digit
arithmetic.
\end{proof}

\begin{figure}[tb]
\centering
\includegraphics[width=\textwidth]{fig_tikz_hexloop}
\caption{The curve of \cref{prop:hexagon-loop}, drawn at $\theta=17^\circ$.
(a) The plane $P$: the six roots of the hexagon turned by $\theta$ from the
reference direction (dotted), the hexagon $\mathrm{Hex}_\theta$ of inradius
$1$ that they cut out, the directions in $P$ of the other eighteen roots,
at radius $\sigma$ (filled: the triangle $T_+$ at $30^\circ,150^\circ,270^\circ$;
open: $T_-$), and the sector between the rays at $30^\circ$ and $90^\circ$,
split at the vertex $v$ into the two triangles (blue and green) over which
$J_1$ and $J_2$ are computed. (b) The slice over a point of
$\mathrm{Hex}_\theta$: the intersection of two opposite triangles of inradii
$h_+$ and $h_-$ in $P'$, a hexagon while $h_+\le2h_-$ and $h_-\le2h_+$. (c) The
cell as the union of its slices over $\mathrm{Hex}_\theta$, with the exact
slices over three points; the two-dimensional slices are drawn in one plane
transverse to $P$. (d) Along the curve: the tilt $\sin\psi$ of the eighteen
roots and the largest inner product, which exceeds $\frac12$ for
$0<\theta<\pi/3$ and reaches $\frac47$ at $\theta=\pi/6$.}
\label{fig:hexloop}
\end{figure}

\begin{remark}[What the curve means]\label{rem:contact-valley}
Through each of the $16$ hexagons of $A_2$ roots there is such a curve. So
the root system is not a strict local minimum of $\vol(Q_w)$ up to
rotations, and no positivity of higher-order terms can prove
$\vol(Q_w)\ge8$ near it. Along straight lines $tk$ in the flat directions the
volume grows at fourth order, $(\vol Q_w-8)/t^4$ between $0.05$ and $0.23$ for
random unit $k$. A minimisation that lets the other $57$ tilt coordinates
move finds this curve. At the
points $\theta=0.1$, $0.3$ and $\pi/6$ of it the Hessian of $\vol(Q_w)$ on the
$72$ tangent directions is positive semidefinite, with seven zero
eigenvalues (the rotations and the curve) and the next $0.0055$, $0.031$ and
$0.045$. Thus numerically the curve is the floor of a valley. Its contact
cells have $78$ vertices, six of them on six facets, where the $24$-cell has
$24$. Along the curve the directions are not a contact configuration, since
the largest inner product reaches $\frac47$ at $\theta=\pi/6$. In a packing
such directions need push-outs, which \cref{prop:push-integrated} turns into
volume above $8$. A proof of $\vol(Q_w)\ge8$ near the root system therefore
has to control the volume near these curves, or use the packing constraint
there.
\end{remark}

Away from the root system the bound $\vol(Q_w)\ge8$ can hold only for a
small slack. We minimised $\vol(Q_w)$
over $24$ unit directions with inner products at most $\tfrac12+s$, from
$48$ random starts spread into codes of that slack and eight perturbations
of the root system, with the exact gradient $-A_i^wc_i$ ($c_i$ the centroid
of the $i$-th facet). For $s=0.005$ and $0.01$ every start that reaches a
code ends at the root system or on a curve of \cref{prop:hexagon-loop},
with volume $8$. For $s=0.02$ the codes it finds away from the root system
have larger cells, the least $8.0679$. For $s=0.03$ and $0.035$ they have
cells of volume $7.99802$ and $7.99288$. Codes of that slack are the
directions of packings only with push-outs, and these compensate for the
difference many times over, so the bound needed is on the whole cell, not on
$Q_w$ (\cref{rem:what-g-needs}).

Statement (i) of \cref{sec:closure} fails well before the slack at
which $25$-point codes appear.

\begin{prop}[A second twenty-four-point code]\label{prop:second-code}
There are $24$ points of $S^3$ with rational coordinates whose pairwise inner
products are at most $\tfrac12+\tfrac{849}{50000}=0.51698$, and whose set $W$
has $d(W)\ge\tfrac{57}{500}$, with $d$ as in \cref{thm:local-uniqueness}.
\end{prop}

\begin{proof}
The points are listed in the supplementary data; each is the inverse
stereographic image of a rational point of $\mathbb{R}^3$, so its norm is
exactly $1$. In exact arithmetic, and again in Lean, every
two of them have inner product at most $\tfrac12+\tfrac{849}{50000}$, and
the inner product $-0.72817\ldots$ of the ninth and eleventh is at distance
at least $\tfrac{57}{250}$ from each of $-1,-\tfrac12,0,\tfrac12,1$. If an
orthogonal map and a relabelling moved the points by $e_i$ onto the
normalised roots, with $\sum e_i^2=d(W)^2$, every inner product would lie
within $e_i+e_j\le2d(W)$ of one of those five values; so
$d(W)\ge\tfrac{57}{500}$.
\end{proof}

The code is not an accident of rounding. Of $64$ random configurations,
spread into codes of slack $0.03$ and then pushed to the least possible
largest inner product, three return to the root system, and the other $59$
all stop at the same value
$0.516978$, a code with no antipodal pair, $84$ pairs at that inner product
and five kinds of point. Its contact cell has volume $8.0948$; realised as
the directions of a packing with all centres at one distance, it needs that
distance to be at least $2.0349$, which leaves a cell of volume at least
$8.674$. Therefore it does not threaten
\cref{conj:shell}, but it limits how the conjecture can be proved:
twenty-four directions of slack $0.017$ or more need not lie near the root
system, and any argument that first places them there stops at that slack.

\begin{remark}[What the twenty-four-centre case needs]\label{rem:what-g-needs}
Let $Y$ be the centres within $\sqrt6$ of $c$ and $T(Y)$ the right side of
\cref{lem:no-triple}. Whatever the rest of the packing does,
$\vol(V_c)\ge T(Y)$ by that lemma and $\vol(V_c)\ge\vol(V(Y)\cap K(Y))$ by
\cref{prop:inversion-hull}. Hence the case of \cref{conj:shell} with exactly
twenty-four centres within $\sqrt6$ follows from
\begin{quote}
(G)\quad $\vol\bigl(V(Y)\cap K(Y)\bigr)\ge8$ for every packing set $Y$ of $24$
centres with $2\le|y|<\sqrt6$ and $T(Y)\le8$, with equality only at the root
system,
\end{quote}
a statement about $24$ points in which statements (i) and (ii) of
\cref{sec:closure} enter together, and which \cref{prop:second-code} does
not touch. The condition $T(Y)\le8$ cannot be dropped. When centres move
out towards $\sqrt6$ the inversion hull shrinks, and there is a packing with
$\vol(V(Y)\cap K(Y))=4.750$; there $T(Y)=10.82$. With the condition, the
constrained minimisation (under the packing constraints and $T(Y)\le8$) finds
nothing below $8$. None of $40$ random configurations can be brought into
the region $T(Y)\le8$, in line with the survey of \cref{sec:closure}, where
every local minimum of $T$ with $24$ centres is the root system. Of $64$
starts made inside it, with tilts up to $0.3$ and up to four centres pushed
out, one as far as $2.19$, the $20$ that stay in the region all end at the
root system with volume $8$. No proof of (G) is known: it is the part of
\cref{conj:shell} that the pair terms and the inversion hull leave between
them, and \cref{rem:what-g-needs-2} sets out what a proof needs.
\end{remark}

For twenty-five or more centres within $\sqrt6$ the pair terms alone
suffice from thirty-one centres on (\cref{thm:count31}). From twenty-five
to thirty they are not proved to suffice, although the least value of $T$
found numerically for such configurations is $8.264$ (\cref{fig:counts}). Together with (G), the counts
from twenty-five to thirty are all that stands between the theorems of this
section and \cref{conj:shell}.

\begin{prop}[The two statements that remain]\label{prop:reduction-G}
Let $T(Y)$ denote the right side of \cref{lem:no-triple} for a set $Y$ of
centres within $\sqrt6$ of $c$. Suppose that
\begin{enumerate}[leftmargin=2.6em]
\item[\textup{(G)}] for every packing set $Y$ of exactly $24$ centres with
  $2\le|y|<\sqrt6$ and $T(Y)\le8$ one has $\vol(V(Y)\cap K(Y))\ge8$, with
  equality only if $Y$ is a copy of $\sqrt2\,\Rt$ about $c$; and
\item[\textup{(C)}] for every packing set $Y$ of at least $25$ centres with
  $2\le|y|<\sqrt6$ one has $T(Y)>8$.
\end{enumerate}
Then \cref{conj:shell} holds: every Voronoi cell of a unit-ball packing of
$\mathbb{R}^4$ has volume at least $8$, with equality only for the
$24$-cell of a copy of $\sqrt2\,\Rt$.
\end{prop}

\begin{proof}
Let $Y$ be the set of centres other than $c$ within $\sqrt6$ of it. Only
these have nonempty caps in $B(\sqrt{3/2})$, so \cref{lem:no-triple} gives
$\vol(V_c)\ge\vol(V_c\cap B(\sqrt{3/2}))=T(Y)$, and
\cref{prop:inversion-hull} gives $\vol(V_c)\ge\vol(V(Y)\cap K(Y))$. If
$|Y|\le23$, then $\vol(V_c)>8$ by \cref{thm:m23-labelled}. If
$|Y|\ge25$, then $\vol(V_c)\ge T(Y)>8$ by (C). If $|Y|=24$ and
$T(Y)>8$, then $\vol(V_c)>8$. If $|Y|=24$ and $T(Y)\le8$, then
$\vol(V_c)\ge8$ by (G), and equality forces $Y$ to be a copy of
$\sqrt2\,\Rt$; then $c$ has $24$ contacts, and $V_c$ is the $24$-cell by
\cref{lem:24-no-shell}.
\end{proof}

\begin{figure}[tb]
\centering
\includegraphics[width=\textwidth]{fig_tikz_codes}
\caption{Twenty-four-point codes at positive slack, and the two statements
that remain. (a) The inner products of the $276$ pairs of the root system and
of the second code of \cref{prop:second-code}. (b) The deepest hole of a
$24$-point code of slack $s$, the least value of $\max_i\langle\theta,w_i\rangle$
found; a further centre within $\sqrt6$ beside twenty-four within $2.0161$
needs $0.6141$ or less, which the codes found reach only near $s=0.025$,
beyond the slack $0.008$ of \cref{thm:kissing-stable} and the slack $0.01698$
of the second code. (c) The least contact-cell volume found over codes of
slack $s$. (d) The cases of \cref{prop:reduction-G}; the green boxes are
proved, the orange ones are statement (G) and statement (C) for twenty-five
to thirty centres, (C) being proved from thirty-one on by \cref{thm:count31}.
Panels (b) and (c) are floating-point minimisations, evidence and not
bounds.}
\label{fig:codes}
\end{figure}

\subsection{The statements (G) and (C)}\label{sec:G-and-C}

This subsection proves (C) for every count from thirty-one on, reduces it
at thirty to a single configuration, shows that
twenty-four centres close to a root system leave no room for another within
$\sqrt6$, proves (G) along the push-outs of the root system, and then sets
out what (G), and (C) for the counts from twenty-five to thirty, still
require. Throughout, $Y$ is the set of centres $y$ with
$2\le|y|<\sqrt6$, $\hat y=y/|y|$, $R=\sqrt{3/2}$, and
\[
  U(Y)=\sum_{y\in Y}S(|y|)-\sum_{\{y,y'\}\subset Y}
  \Pi\bigl(\tfrac{|y|}2,\tfrac{|y'|}2,\langle\hat y,\hat y'\rangle\bigr)
\]
is the volume of the union of the caps of \cref{lem:no-triple}, $\Pi$ being
the pair term of the proof of \cref{thm:m23-labelled}. So
$T(Y)=\frac98\pi^2-U(Y)$, and (C) asks that $U(Y)<\frac98\pi^2-8=3.10330\ldots$
whenever $|Y|\ge25$.

\begin{lem}[The pair term]\label{lem:pair-closed}
Let $1\le h_1,h_2<R$ and $-1<u<1$. Put $\gamma=\arccos u$,
$\alpha_k=\arccos(h_k/R)$, $L=\max(-\alpha_1,\gamma-\alpha_2)$,
$U=\min(\alpha_1,\gamma+\alpha_2)$, let $\phi_c$ be the angle of the vector
$(h_1\sin\gamma,\,h_2-h_1\cos\gamma)$, clipped to $[L,U]$, and let
$G(h,x)=R^4x-2R^2h^2\tan x+h^4(\tan x+\frac13\tan^3x)$. Then
\[
  \Pi(h_1,h_2,u)=\tfrac\pi4\bigl(G(h_2,\phi_c-\gamma)-G(h_2,L-\gamma)
  +G(h_1,U)-G(h_1,\phi_c)\bigr)
\]
if $L<U$, and $\Pi(h_1,h_2,u)=0$ otherwise. Moreover $\Pi$ is nonincreasing
in $h_1$ and in $h_2$, and nondecreasing in $u$.
\end{lem}

\begin{proof}
Write $x=(p,q)$ with $p$ in the plane $P$ of the two normals $w_1,w_2$ and
$q\perp P$. The two conditions $\langle x,w_k\rangle>h_k$ involve $p$ alone,
and for fixed $p$ the condition $|x|<R$ leaves a disc of area
$\pi(R^2-|p|^2)$ for $q$; so $\Pi=\int\pi(R^2-|p|^2)\,dp$ over the part of
$P$ beyond both lines. In polar coordinates $p=\rho(\cos\phi,\sin\phi)$,
with $w_1$ at $\phi=0$ and $w_2$ at $\phi=\gamma$, the admissible $\rho$ run
from $\rho_m(\phi)=\max\bigl(h_1/\cos\phi,\,h_2/\cos(\phi-\gamma)\bigr)$ to
$R$, and $\int_{\rho_m}^R\pi(R^2-\rho^2)\rho\,d\rho=\frac\pi4(R^2-\rho_m^2)^2$.
This is positive exactly for $L<\phi<U$. The two terms of the maximum cross
once, at $\tan\phi=(h_2-h_1\cos\gamma)/(h_1\sin\gamma)$; the second line
binds before the crossing and the first after it, and
$\int\frac\pi4(R^2-h^2\sec^2x)^2\,dx=\frac\pi4G(h,x)$. For the monotonicity,
read the same integral circle by circle. On the circle of radius $\rho$ the
admissible angles form the intersection of the arcs
$(-\beta_1,\beta_1)$ and $(\gamma-\beta_2,\gamma+\beta_2)$, where
$\beta_k=\arccos(h_k/\rho)<\frac\pi2$; the second arc lies in
$(-\frac\pi2,\frac{3\pi}2)$ and meets the first only inside
$(-\frac\pi2,\frac\pi2)$, so the length of the intersection is
$\max(0,\min(\beta_1,\gamma+\beta_2)-\max(-\beta_1,\gamma-\beta_2))$, which
does not increase when $h_1$, $h_2$ or $\gamma\in[0,\pi]$ increases. The
weight $\pi(R^2-\rho^2)\rho$ depends on none of them.
\end{proof}

\begin{thm}[Thirty-one or more centres]\label{thm:count31}
If at least $31$ centres other than $c$ lie within $\sqrt6$ of $c$, then
$U(Y)<3.0854$, and so $\vol(V_c)\ge T(Y)>8.0179$. In particular, statement
(C) of \cref{prop:reduction-G} holds for every packing set of at least $31$
centres.
\end{thm}

\begin{proof}
For $2\le d\le\sqrt6$ let $p(d)=(T_0(x),\dots,T_r(x))$, where the $T_a$ are
the Chebyshev polynomials and $x=(2d-c_1)/c_2$ for rationals $c_1,c_2$ close
to $2+\sqrt6$ and $\sqrt6-2$; let $U_k$ be the Chebyshev polynomials of the
second kind, which are the zonal polynomials of $S^3$ \cite{DGS77}. For
symmetric matrices $A_0,\dots,A_D$ put
\[
  K(y,y')=\sum_{k=0}^{D}\frac{U_k(\langle\hat y,\hat y'\rangle)}{k+1}\;
  p(|y|)^{\mathsf T}A_k\,p(|y'|).
\]
If $A_1,\dots,A_D$ are positive semidefinite, then for every finite set $Y$
each term with $k\ge1$ contributes a nonnegative amount to
$\sum_{y,y'\in Y}K(y,y')$, by Schoenberg's theorem \cite{Sch42} applied to
each eigenvector of $A_k$, and the term with $k=0$ contributes
$v^{\mathsf T}A_0v$, where $v=\sum_{y\in Y}p(|y|)$. Suppose further that
\begin{enumerate}[label=\textup{(\alph*)},leftmargin=2.2em]
\item $K(y,y')\le\Pi\bigl(\frac{|y|}2,\frac{|y'|}2,\langle\hat y,\hat y'\rangle\bigr)$
  whenever $2\le|y|,|y'|\le\sqrt6$ and
  $\langle\hat y,\hat y'\rangle\le a(|y|,|y'|)$, the condition \eqref{eq:amax}
  for $|y-y'|\ge2$;
\item $\begin{pmatrix}A_0&z\\ z^{\mathsf T}&t\end{pmatrix}$ is positive
  semidefinite for some vector $z$ and number $t$;
\item $S(d)+\frac12\sum_kp(d)^{\mathsf T}A_kp(d)-z\cdot p(d)\le m$ for
  $2\le d\le\sqrt6$, with $m<0$.
\end{enumerate}
Summing (a) over ordered pairs of distinct centres gives
$2\sum_{\{y,y'\}}\Pi\ge v^{\mathsf T}A_0v-\sum_yK(y,y)$, and (b) gives
$-\frac12v^{\mathsf T}A_0v\le-z\cdot v+\frac t2$. Since $K(y,y)$ is the sum
in (c),
\[
  U(Y)\le\sum_{y\in Y}\Bigl(S(|y|)+\tfrac12K(y,y)-z\cdot p(|y|)\Bigr)+\tfrac t2
  \le|Y|\,m+\tfrac t2 ,
\]
and the right side decreases as $|Y|$ grows. The certificate has $D=12$ and
$r=4$, $m=-0.072797\ldots$, and $31m+\frac t2=3.08537\ldots$, below
$\frac98\pi^2-8$. The semidefinite programme that finds it imposes (a) at
about $41\,000$ sampled triples with a margin of $10^{-5}$; the proof does not
use the samples. The matrices are rounded to dyadic rationals and are
positive semidefinite by an exact $LDL^{\mathsf T}$, and $t$ is set to
$z^{\mathsf T}A_0^{-1}z$ rounded up, which makes (b) exact. For (a), $K$ is a
polynomial in $(|y|,|y'|,u)$; its tensor Bernstein coefficients on
$[2,d_{\max}]^2\times[-1,a(d_{\max},d_{\max})]$, with
$d_{\max}=2.4494898>\sqrt6$, are computed exactly and subdivided at
midpoints, and on each box the largest coefficient bounds $K$, while by
\cref{lem:pair-closed} $\Pi$ is at least its value at the corner with the
larger distances and the smaller inner product, computed from the closed form
in ball arithmetic \cite{Joh17}. Boxes with $|y|>|y'|$ ($K$ and $\Pi$ are
symmetric) or lying wholly beyond $u=a(|y|,|y'|)$ are set aside by exact
rational tests, and the branch and bound closes (a) with $191\,740$ boxes.
Condition (c) is checked in the same way on $356$ intervals, $S$ being
decreasing, and the final inequality in ball arithmetic.
\end{proof}

With at most $23$ centres within $\sqrt6$ the bound holds by
\cref{thm:m23-labelled}, and with $31$ or more by \cref{thm:count31}
(\cref{fig:count31}); what remains of (C) is the range from $25$ to $30$.

\begin{remark}[Where the kernel stops]\label{rem:kernel-limit}
The argument is Delsarte's linear programming method \cite{DGS77}, with the
distance from $c$ carried along as a continuous label, and it is blind in
the same way: a kernel on pairs alone bounds the kissing number of
$\mathbb{R}^4$ by $25$ and not $24$ \cite{OS79}. \Cref{tab:kernel-limit}
gives, for each count, the least value of $|Y|m+\frac t2$ that such a
certificate reaches, against the largest union the search of
\cref{fig:counts} finds. Below $31$ the kernel cannot separate the
configurations that exist from those it allows; at $25$ it does not even
reach the value $3.19636\ldots$ of the root system, which has only
twenty-four centres. Adding to the programme the bound of
\cref{thm:kissing-stable}, at most $24$ centres within $2.0161$, leaves these
values unchanged.
A kernel on triples in the manner of \cite{BV08}, with the distances as
labels and each anchor weighted by a nonnegative polynomial in its own
distance, does better at thirty centres, but not well enough. At angular
degree $6$, three rounds of cutting planes raise the value of its programme
to $3.10344$, already above $3.10330$, and further rounds can only raise it.
A proof along that line would also need more than the value: each inequality
on distinct triples enters the bound once for each of the
$30\cdot29\cdot28=24\,360$ ordered triples, so it would have to be verified to
within about $10^{-8}$ on a six-dimensional domain on which the optimal kernel
stays within $10^{-5}$ of zero on half of the sampled triples.
\end{remark}

Twenty-four centres close to a root system leave no room for a
twenty-fifth. The proof of \cref{lem:24-no-shell} shows that every unit
vector makes an angle of at most $45^\circ$ with some normalised root. The
following versions keep a margin, measured direction by direction, in
root-sum-square, and through the inner products alone.

\begin{cor}[No room beside a near root system]\label{cor:no-room}
Let $2\le\rho<\sqrt6$, put $\tau=a(\sqrt6,\rho)$ and $h=1/\sqrt2-\tau$, and
let $24$ centres $y_1,\dots,y_{24}$ lie within $\rho$ of $c$. Suppose that,
for a labelling of the roots $a_i$ and, in \textup{(i)} and \textup{(ii)},
an orthogonal map $\sigma$, one of the following holds:
\begin{enumerate}[label=\textup{(\roman*)},leftmargin=2.2em]
\item $|\hat y_i-\sigma(a_i)/\sqrt2|<h$ for every $i$;
\item $\sum_i|\hat y_i-\sigma(a_i)/\sqrt2|^2<6h^2$;
\item $\rho=2/\sqrt{1-0.016}$ and
  $\sum_{i<j}\bigl(\langle\hat y_i,\hat y_j\rangle-\tfrac12\langle a_i,a_j\rangle\bigr)^2\le0.3059$.
\end{enumerate}
Then no other centre lies within $\sqrt6$ of $c$. For
$\rho=2/\sqrt{1-0.016}=2.0161\ldots$ one has $\tau=0.614038\ldots$ and
$h=0.093068\ldots$, so \textup{(i)} asks for less than $0.0930$ in each
direction and \textup{(ii)} for a root-sum-square distance below
$\sqrt6\,h=0.22796\ldots$. If $24$ centres lie within
$2/\sqrt{1-4\cdot10^{-26}}$ of $c$, no other centre lies within $\sqrt6$
of $c$.
\end{cor}

\begin{proof}
Let $z$ be a further centre with $|z|<\sqrt6$. By \eqref{eq:amax}, and since
$a$ increases in each argument on $[2,\sqrt6]^2$,
$\langle\hat z,\hat y_i\rangle\le a(|z|,|y_i|)\le\tau$ for every $i$.

(i) The covering radius of the normalised roots is $45^\circ$, so some $i$
has $\langle\hat z,\sigma(a_i)/\sqrt2\rangle\ge1/\sqrt2$, and then
$\langle\hat z,\hat y_i\rangle>1/\sqrt2-h=\tau$, a contradiction.

(ii) Put $t_i=\langle\hat z,\sigma(a_i)/\sqrt2\rangle$. Since $\hat z$ is a
unit vector, $|\hat y_i-\sigma(a_i)/\sqrt2|\ge
t_i-\langle\hat z,\hat y_i\rangle\ge t_i-\tau$, so
\begin{equation}\label{eq:hole-budget}
  \sum_i|\hat y_i-\sigma(a_i)/\sqrt2|^2\ \ge\ \sum_i(t_i-\tau)_+^2 .
\end{equation}
The normalised roots are the vectors $(\pm e_j\pm e_k)/\sqrt2$, and
expanding over them gives $\sum_it_i^2=6$ and $\sum_it_i^4=3$ for every unit
vector: the root system is a spherical $5$-design. The roots come in pairs
$\pm a$, and at most one member of a pair has $t_i>0$; so the right side of
\eqref{eq:hole-budget} is $\sum_k(s_k-\tau)_+^2$ over twelve numbers
$s_k\in[0,1]$, one for each pair, with $\sum_ks_k^2=3$ and
$\sum_ks_k^4=\frac32$. Put $\gamma=2\sqrt2\,h-4h^2$, $\beta=\sqrt2\,h-\gamma$
and $q(s)=(s-\tau)_+^2-\beta s^2-\gamma s^4$. On $[0,\tau]$,
$q(s)\ge-s^2(\beta+\gamma\tau^2)$, and
$\beta+\gamma\tau^2=-2h^2(1-3\sqrt2\,h+2h^2)<0$. On $[\tau,1]$, $q$ is a
quartic with $q(1/\sqrt2)=q'(1/\sqrt2)=0$ by the choice of $\beta$ and
$\gamma$, so $q(s)=(s-1/\sqrt2)^2r(s)$ with $r$ quadratic. Its leading
coefficient is $-\gamma<0$, and it is positive at both ends, since
$r(\tau)=-\tau^2(\beta+\gamma\tau^2)/h^2$ and
$(1-1/\sqrt2)^2\,r(1)=(1-\tau)^2-\beta-\gamma
=(h-\tfrac1{\sqrt2})(h-\tfrac{3\sqrt2-4}2)$. These signs hold for
$0<h<\frac{3\sqrt2-4}2=0.1213\ldots$, and here
$h\le1/\sqrt2-a(\sqrt6,2)=0.0947\ldots$; the identities and the signs are
checked again in Lean. Thus $q\ge0$ on $[0,1]$, and
\[
  \sum_k(s_k-\tau)_+^2\ \ge\ \beta\sum_ks_k^2+\gamma\sum_ks_k^4
  \ =\ 3\beta+\tfrac32\gamma\ =\ 6h^2,
\]
against \textup{(ii)}. The bound is attained: at a deep hole of the root
system six of the $t_i$ equal $1/\sqrt2$ and the others are at most $0$.

(iii) Let $X$ and $V$ be the $24\times4$ matrices with rows $\hat y_i$ and
$a_i/\sqrt2$, so that $V^{\mathsf T}V=6I$ and $P=VV^{\mathsf T}/6$ is the
orthogonal projection onto the column space of $V$. Put $A=X^{\mathsf T}X$,
$E=X^{\mathsf T}(I-P)X$, $e=\operatorname{tr}E$ and $\alpha=\|A-6I\|$, all
norms being Frobenius norms. Since $\operatorname{tr}A=24$ and
$\|V^{\mathsf T}X\|^2=6\operatorname{tr}(X^{\mathsf T}PX)=144-6e$,
\[
  \|XX^{\mathsf T}-VV^{\mathsf T}\|^2=\|A\|^2+144-2\|V^{\mathsf T}X\|^2
  =\alpha^2+12e ,
\]
and the left side is twice the sum in \textup{(iii)}, the diagonals being
equal. For an orthogonal $Q$, $\|X-VQ\|^2=e+\|PX-VQ\|^2=e+6\|M/6-Q\|^2$ with
$M=V^{\mathsf T}X$, and the least value over $Q$ is
$e+\frac16\sum_k(\mu_k-6)^2$, where the $\mu_k$ are the singular values of
$M$. As $M^{\mathsf T}M=6(A-E)$, $\mu_k=\sqrt{6\lambda_k}$ for the
eigenvalues $\lambda_k$ of $A-E$, and these lie within
$\|A-E-6I\|\le\alpha+e$ of $6$, because $\|E\|\le\operatorname{tr}E$ for
$E\succeq0$. Hence
\[
  \min_Q\|X-VQ\|^2=e+\sum_k\bigl(\sqrt{\lambda_k}-\sqrt6\bigr)^2
  \ \le\ e+\frac{(\alpha+e)^2}{\bigl(\sqrt{6-\alpha-e}+\sqrt6\bigr)^2}.
\]
The right side increases in $\alpha$ and in $e$. Splitting
$[0,2\cdot0.3059/12]$ into $200$ intervals of $e$ and bounding the square
roots by rationals, one checks in exact arithmetic, and again in Lean, that it stays below
$0.0515$ whenever $\alpha^2+12e\le2\cdot0.3059$, while $6h^2>0.05197$ at
$\rho=2/\sqrt{1-0.016}$. Therefore \textup{(ii)} holds with
$\sigma(x)=Q^{\mathsf T}x$ for the minimising $Q$.

For the last statement, the directions of the $24$ centres have pairwise
inner products at most $a(\rho,\rho)=\frac12+2\cdot10^{-26}$, so
\cref{thm:m24-robust} places them within $0.0113$ of a copy of the
normalised roots, and \textup{(i)} applies, as
$0.0113<1/\sqrt2-a(\sqrt6,2.0001)$.
\end{proof}

Parts (ii) and (iii) compare the directions with the roots through a
labelling. The next statement needs none: it asks only that the twenty-four
directions be close to a spherical $5$-design, measured by the Gegenbauer
sums
\[
  S_k=\sum_{i,j=1}^{24}G_k\bigl(\langle\hat y_i,\hat y_j\rangle\bigr),\qquad
  k=1,\dots,5,
\]
where $G_k=U_k/(k+1)$, with $U_k$ the Chebyshev polynomial of the second kind,
is the Gegenbauer polynomial of $S^3$ normalised by $G_k(1)=1$. Each $S_k$ is
nonnegative, and all five vanish for the root system in every position,
since it is a spherical $5$-design.

\begin{prop}[No room from the design defects]\label{prop:design-budget}
Let $24$ centres $y_1,\dots,y_{24}$ lie within $\rho=2/\sqrt{1-0.016}$ of
$c$. If
\[
  \tfrac1{963}\bigl(103\,S_1+191\,S_2+280\,S_3+230\,S_4+159\,S_5\bigr)<0.2678,
\]
then no other centre lies within $\sqrt6$ of $c$.
\end{prop}

\begin{proof}
Write $c_1,\dots,c_5$ for the weights $103/963,\dots,159/963$, and let
$p=\sum_{k=0}^5p_kG_k$ with
\[
  (p_0,\dots,p_5)=\Bigl(\tfrac{39954677}{453230000000},\ \tfrac{41}{89653},\
  \tfrac{29}{33806},\ \tfrac{17}{14206},\ \tfrac{95}{98183},\
  \tfrac{52}{86257}\Bigr).
\]
Then $p(t)<0$ for $-1\le t\le0.614039$: on each of $29$ subintervals covering
that range, a Taylor bound about the midpoint, computed in rational
arithmetic, is negative. Moreover $0.614039>\tau=a(\sqrt6,\rho)$, since
$\tau^2=(2+\rho^2)^2/(24\rho^2)=139129/369000$.

Let $z$ be a further centre with $|z|<\sqrt6$. As in the proof of
\cref{cor:no-room}, $\langle\hat z,\hat y_i\rangle\le\tau$ for every $i$,
so $\sum_ip(\langle\hat z,\hat y_i\rangle)<0$. On the other hand, the
addition formula $G_k(\langle x,y\rangle)=h_k^{-1}\sum_mY_{km}(x)Y_{km}(y)$,
with $h_k=(k+1)^2$ and an orthonormal basis $Y_{km}$ of the harmonics of
degree $k$, and the Cauchy--Schwarz inequality give
\[
  \Bigl|\sum_iG_k(\langle\hat z,\hat y_i\rangle)\Bigr|
  =h_k^{-1}\Bigl|\sum_mY_{km}(\hat z)\sum_iY_{km}(\hat y_i)\Bigr|
  \le\sqrt{S_k},
\]
because $\sum_mY_{km}(\hat z)^2=h_k$ and
$\sum_m\bigl(\sum_iY_{km}(\hat y_i)\bigr)^2=h_kS_k$. Hence
\[
  \sum_ip(\langle\hat z,\hat y_i\rangle)
  \ \ge\ 24p_0-\sum_{k=1}^5|p_k|\sqrt{S_k}
  \ \ge\ 24p_0-\Bigl(\sum_k\frac{p_k^2}{c_k}\Bigr)^{1/2}
          \Bigl(\sum_kc_kS_k\Bigr)^{1/2}.
\]
In exact arithmetic $(24p_0)^2>0.26785\sum_kp_k^2/c_k$, so under the
hypothesis the right side is positive, a contradiction. The sign of $p$, the
inequality $0.614039>\tau$, the bound $0.26785$ and the vanishing of
$S_1,\dots,S_5$ for the root system are checked again in Lean.
\end{proof}

\begin{remark}[What (C) needs from twenty-five to thirty centres]\label{rem:what-c-needs}
Let $Y$ be a packing set of $M$ centres, $25\le M\le30$, with
$U(Y)\ge3.10330$. Since $U(Y)\le\sum_yS(|y|)$ and $S$ falls steeply,
from $S(2)=0.13896$ to $S(2.0161)=0.12733$, $S(2.1)=0.07581$ and
$S(2.2)=0.03340$, most of the union must come from centres close to $c$: if
at most $22$ centres lay within $\rho$, then
$U(Y)\le22S(2)+(M-22)S(\rho)$, which is below $3.10330$ once
$S(\rho)<0.00576$, that is, once $\rho\ge2.328$ for $M=30$ and
$\rho\ge2.268$ for $M=25$. Hence at least $23$ centres lie within
$2.328$, and by \cref{thm:kissing-stable} at most $24$ within $2.0161$. The
two cases differ in kind.

With $24$ centres close to $c$, the others must find room beside them, and
\cref{cor:no-room} says there is none as soon as the $24$ directions are
within $0.093$ each of a root system, or within $0.228$ of one in
root-sum-square. A centre within $\sqrt6$ beside twenty-four centres within
$2.0161$ needs a hole of their directions at inner product $0.6141$ or less;
the local searches behind \cref{fig:codes}(b) find no $24$-point code of
slack up to $0.02$ with a hole deeper than $0.632$, and at slack $0.008$
none deeper than $0.678$. What this case needs is therefore the shape half
of \cref{thm:kissing-stable} in a weak form, with a tolerance of $0.228$ in
root-sum-square in place of the $\frac1{48}$ in root mean square of
\cref{thm:near-contact}. The relaxations at hand do not give it. A
three-point certificate on the directions does not detect holes: with a
further direction at inner product at most $0.6141$ with each of $n$
directions of slack $0.008$, no labelled certificate of degree $7$, $10$ or
$12$ excludes $n=24$. The second level of the hierarchy determines
twenty-four points exactly \cite{LLM24}, but not with a margin on pairs,
which the root system itself uses up (\cref{prop:margin-floor}), and at
slack $0.008$ its value is $24.555$, more than half a point above $24$.
\Cref{prop:design-budget} reduces the case to a bound on five weighted
design defects, which the codes of slack $0.008$ found by local search meet
with room, at $0.0905$ against the $0.2678$ allowed, but which no relaxation
we have run proves.

With $23$ centres close to $c$, the case is \cref{thm:m23-labelled} with
two or more further centres, and \cref{thm:certificate} has a margin of only
$4.4\cdot10^{-5}$ there, against $S(2.2)=0.0334$ for each further centre
within $2.2$; so the further centres must be compensated by their own pair
terms with the $23$. Packings leave room here: minimised with at least $23$
centres held within $\rho$, $T$ does not come below $8.353$, $8.472$,
$8.681$, $8.781$, $8.771$ and $8.937$ for $M=25,\dots,30$. The relaxations do
not see that room. The root system with one centre pushed out along its ray
to a distance between $2.0161$ and $2.197$, together with a fictitious
centre near $\sqrt6$ in one of its holes, has a union of caps at least
$\frac98\pi^2-8$; the fictitious centre needs inner products only up to
$0.612$ with the others, and neither the kernel of \cref{thm:count31} nor a
three-point kernel on the directions alone tells this configuration from a
packing. A proof of this case has to use the distances, through $T$, as (G)
does. What the pair terms give is \cref{lem:twentyfour-close}: when $T\le8$,
twenty-four of the centres lie within $2.444$, so that the case of
twenty-three close centres is again a question about the twenty-four
closest.

The values in this remark other than those of
\cref{cor:no-room,prop:design-budget,thm:kissing-stable} are optima of
semidefinite programmes or results of local searches, computed in floating
point. They show where the methods stop and are used in no proof.
\end{remark}

\begin{lem}[Twenty-four centres within $2.444$]\label{lem:twentyfour-close}
Let $Y$ be a packing set of $M$ centres with $2\le|y|<\sqrt6$, where
$25\le M\le33$, and suppose that $T(Y)\le8$. Then at least $24$ centres of
$Y$ lie within $2.444$ of $c$. If $Z$ consists of the $24$ centres of $Y$
closest to $c$ and $d_{25}$ is the distance of the next, then
$T(Z)\le8+(M-24)\,S(d_{25})$.
\end{lem}

\begin{proof}
For $X\subseteq Y$ the union of the caps of $Y$ is at most that of $X$
together with the caps of the centres of $Y\setminus X$, so
\begin{equation}\label{eq:subset-T}
  T(X)\ \le\ T(Y)+\sum_{y\in Y\setminus X}S(|y|).
\end{equation}
If at most $22$ centres lie within $2.444$, then, $S$ being decreasing,
$U(Y)\le\sum_yS(|y|)\le22S(2)+11S(2.444)=3.05722\ldots<\frac98\pi^2-8$,
and $T(Y)>8$. Suppose that exactly $23$ lie within $2.444$, and let $X$ be
these. The proof of \cref{thm:m23-labelled} bounds $T(X)$ from below for
any $23$ centres at distances in $[2,\sqrt6)$: if one of them is at
distance $D=2.1648$ or more, Step~1 gives $T(X)\ge A_*+s(D)$, and otherwise
Step~2 gives $T(X)\ge A_*+B$. As $s(D)=0.0928820\ldots<B$, in either case
$T(X)\ge A_*+s(D)=8+2.6491\ldots\cdot10^{-5}$. The other $M-23\le10$
centres lie beyond $2.444$, where $S(2.444)=2.5324\ldots\cdot10^{-6}$, and
\eqref{eq:subset-T} gives
$T(Y)\ge T(X)-10\,S(2.444)\ge8+1.1\cdot10^{-6}>8$. Thus at least $24$
centres lie within $2.444$. The last statement is \eqref{eq:subset-T} with
$X=Z$, the $M-24$ other centres being at distance at least $d_{25}$. The
constants are computed in ball arithmetic at $200$ bits;
\cref{fig:twentyfour-close} shows them.
\end{proof}

With $34$ centres the same argument would need $11\,S(2.444)$, which
exceeds the room $2.6491\cdot10^{-5}$ of \cref{thm:m23-labelled}; that
count is settled by \cref{thm:count31} in any case.

\begin{figure}[tb]
\centering
\includegraphics[width=\textwidth]{fig_tikz_lemma}
\caption{The constants of \cref{lem:twentyfour-close}. (a) The volume $S(d)$
of the cap of a centre at distance $d$, which vanishes at $\sqrt6$, with the
radii of the argument: $2.0161$ of \cref{thm:kissing-stable}, $D$ of Step~1 of
\cref{thm:m23-labelled}, $2.4$ of \cref{thm:count30} and $2.444$. (b) The caps
of the $M-23$ centres beyond $2.444$ against the room that
\cref{thm:m23-labelled} leaves above $8$ for the twenty-three closest: they
fit under it for $25\le M\le33$ and not from $M=34$.}
\label{fig:twentyfour-close}
\end{figure}

The kernel of \cref{thm:count31} can be sharpened by splitting into cases
according to how many centres lie within given distances of $c$. At thirty
centres this leaves only configurations of the kind that the first part of
\cref{rem:what-c-needs} describes, near-contacts with further centres in
their holes.

\begin{thm}[Thirty centres]\label{thm:count30}
Suppose that exactly $30$ centres other than $c$ lie within $\sqrt6$ of $c$
and that $T(Y)\le8$. Then at least $22$ of them lie within $2.1$ of $c$, and
at least six lie at distances between $2.4$ and $\sqrt6$.
\end{thm}

\begin{proof}
Let $N(\rho)$ be the number of centres within $\rho$ of $c$; by
\cref{thm:kissing-stable}, $N(2.0161)\le24$. Call a \emph{case} a set of
bounds $\ell_j\le N(\rho_j)\le h_j$ at finitely many rational radii
$2<\rho_1<\dots<\rho_J<\sqrt6$, always including $N(2.0161)\le24$. The radii cut
$[2,d_{\max}]$ into bins; let $n_b$ be the number of centres in bin $b$ and
$e_b$ its lower end. Take a certificate with properties (a) and (b) of the
proof of \cref{thm:count31} and, in place of (c), bounds
\[
  f(d)=S(d)+\tfrac12\textstyle\sum_kp(d)^{\mathsf T}A_kp(d)-z\cdot p(d)\le m_b
  \qquad\text{on each bin } b ,
\]
with no condition on the signs of the $m_b$. The chain of inequalities in that
proof gives $U(Y)\le\sum_bn_bm_b+\frac t2$. If $U(Y)\ge\frac98\pi^2-8$, then
also $\sum_bn_bS(e_b)\ge\sum_yS(|y|)\ge U(Y)\ge\frac98\pi^2-8$, because $S$
decreases and the pair terms are nonnegative; so for every $s\ge0$
\[
  U(Y)\le\sum_bn_b\bigl(m_b+sS(e_b)\bigr)-s\bigl(\tfrac98\pi^2-8\bigr)+\tfrac t2 ,
\]
and $T(Y)>8$ follows once the largest value of the right side over the
integer vectors $(n_b)$ that the case allows, with $\sum_bn_b=30$, is below
$\frac98\pi^2-8$. That largest value is found exactly by dynamic programming
over the bins.

Split first at $N(2.1)\le21$ against $N(2.1)\ge22$, then the second case
at $N(2.4)\le24$ against $N(2.4)\ge25$, then $N(2.1)\ge22$, $N(2.4)\ge25$ at
$N(2.1)\le23$ against $N(2.1)\ge24$, and $22\le N(2.1)\le23$, $N(2.4)\ge25$
at $N(2.2)\le23$ against $N(2.2)\ge24$. The five cases so obtained cover every
packing, and four of them carry certificates, with $D=12$ and $r=4$: the
case $N(2.1)\le21$ with bound $3.0617$, the two cases with $22\le N(2.1)\le23$
and $N(2.4)\ge25$ with $3.0992$ and $3.0968$, and the case $N(2.1)\ge24$,
$N(2.4)\ge25$ with $3.0845$. The fifth, $N(2.1)\ge22$ and $N(2.4)\le24$, is
the one in the statement. Each certificate is checked as in \cref{thm:count31}: exact
positivity, the branch and bound of (a) over the whole admissible domain with
$\Pi$ in ball arithmetic, the bin bounds by Bernstein coefficients (an
interval that meets two bins held to the smaller bound), and the dynamic
programme and the final inequality in ball arithmetic. The four branch
and bounds close $185$ million boxes, $134$ million of them in the tightest
case, and evaluate $\Pi$ in ball arithmetic at $29.6$ million of them.
\end{proof}

\begin{remark}[What is left at thirty]\label{rem:thirty-left}
In the case that \cref{thm:count30} leaves, at most $24$ centres lie within
$2.4$, and by \cref{prop:C-radial} below at least $24$ lie within $2.25$,
$23$ of them within $2.15$ and $22$ within $2.05$. Therefore the configuration
splits into two shells: twenty-four centres within $2.25$ and six beyond
$2.4$, with caps of volume at most $S(2.4)=0.00061$ each, and none in
between. The twenty-four closest therefore carry by themselves a union of
at least $3.0996$, and by \cref{lem:twentyfour-close} their own $T$ is at
most $8+6S(2.4)=8.00368$. And each far centre sits in a
hole of the $22$ to $24$ directions within $2.1$, at inner product at most
$a(2.1,\sqrt6)=0.6231$ with every one of them, while two far centres are at
least $48.2^\circ$ apart. With $24$ directions there, the argument of
\cref{cor:no-room} leaves no room once each is within
$1/\sqrt2-0.6231=0.084$ of a root system. The residual case therefore
reduces to a localisation of the kind (G) asks for
(\cref{rem:what-g-needs-2}), at $T\le8.00368$ in place of $8$, together with
a count of the holes that a nearly complete set of twenty-four directions
leaves. It is also where the two-point kernel stops: the optimum of the
programme on the subcase $22\le N(2.1)\le23$, $N(2.2)=N(2.4)=24$ is
$3.1112$, no single further split at the radii $2.0161$, $2.05$, $2.15$,
$2.2$, $2.25$, $2.3$ or $2.35$ brings both halves below $3.1097$, and on the
subcase $N(2.2)\le23$ degrees up to $16$ in the angle and $6$ in the distance
give $3.1012$, too close to $3.10330$ for a certificate with a usable
margin.\footnote{These are optima of the semidefinite programme computed in
floating point; they are not bounds on $U$, and they are reported only to
show where the two-point method ends.}
\end{remark}

Below thirty centres the tree of \cref{thm:count30} no longer closes its
cases, but a single split still does, at several radii, and at thirty it
adds to the theorem. The counts it gives say where the centres of a
counterexample to (C) must lie.

\begin{prop}[Where the centres lie from twenty-five to thirty]\label{prop:C-radial}
Let $Y$ be a packing set of exactly $M$ centres with $2\le|y|<\sqrt6$, where
$25\le M\le30$, and suppose that $T(Y)\le8$. Then for each radius $\rho$ at
which \cref{tab:C-radial} has an entry in the row of $M$, at least that many
centres of $Y$ lie within $\rho$ of $c$. At least $24$ centres of $Y$ lie
within $2.25$ of $c$ if $M=30$, within $2.3$ if $M=29$, and within $2.35$
if $M=27$ or $28$; and at least $8$ lie within $2.0161$ if $M=29$.
\end{prop}

\begin{proof}
For an entry $n$ at the radius $\rho$, take in the proof of
\cref{thm:count30} the tree with $M$ centres in place of thirty and the
single split $N(\rho)\le n-1$ against $N(\rho)\ge n$. Its first case carries a certificate of degree $12$
in the angle and $4$ in the distance, and the largest value the certificate
gives over the count vectors of that case lies below $\frac98\pi^2-8$; the
largest of these values is $3.10276$, at $M=27$ and $\rho=2.35$. Thus $T(Y)>8$ in
the first case, and $N(\rho)\ge n$ whenever $T(Y)\le8$. An entry that
repeats the one to its left needs no certificate of its own. The last
statement is the split $N(\rho)\le23$ against $N(\rho)\ge24$ at $\rho=2.25$,
$2.3$ and $2.35$, and $N(2.0161)\le7$ against $N(2.0161)\ge8$ at $M=29$. Each
certificate is found and checked as those of \cref{thm:count30}, with the
samples of the programme refined along the antipodal pairs, where the caps
are disjoint and $\Pi$ vanishes.\footnote{The margin
by which the kernel is held below $\Pi$ costs more here than with
twenty-four centres. It is given up at each of the $\binom M2$ pairs, so
that at $M=29$ a margin of $5\cdot10^{-5}$ raises the bound by about
$0.02$. The margins are therefore chosen
case by case, between $2.5\cdot10^{-6}$ and $5\cdot10^{-5}$, according to
the room that the floating-point optimum of the case leaves below the
level.}
\end{proof}

\begin{table}[tb]
\centering
\caption{\Cref{prop:C-radial}. For $M$ centres within $\sqrt6$ of $c$ with
$T(Y)\le8$, the least number of centres within $\rho$ of $c$ that the case

force. A count within one radius holds within every larger one. The row
$24$ restates \cref{prop:G-radial}, and at $2.1$ the row $30$ is
\cref{thm:count30}; a dash means that no count
above the one in parentheses is proved.}
\label{tab:C-radial}

\cmidrule(l){2-5}
$M$ & $2.05$ & $2.1$ & $2.15$ & $2.2$\\
\midrule
$24$ & $20$\,(18) & $22$\,(21) & $23$\,(22) & $23$\,(22)\\
$25$ & $17$\,(15) & $21$\,(20) & $22$\,(21) & $23$\,(22)\\
$26$ & $15$\,(12) & $20$\,(18) & $22$\,(21) & $23$\,(22)\\
$27$ & $14$\,(9) & $20$\,(17) & $21$\,(20) & $22$\,(21)\\
$28$ & --\,(6) & $21$\,(16) & $22$\,(19) & $23$\,(21)\\
$29$ & $19$\,(2) & $22$\,(15) & $23$\,(19) & $23$\,(21)\\
$30$ & $22$\,(0) & $22$\,(14) & $23$\,(18) & $23$\,(20)\\
