\end{table}

The caps alone give the counts in parentheses. Since
$\sum_yS(|y|)\ge U(Y)\ge\frac98\pi^2-8$ when $T(Y)\le8$, and $S$ decreases,
at most $k$ centres can lie within $\rho$ only if
$kS(2)+(M-k)S(\rho)\ge\frac98\pi^2-8$. The pair terms add most where the
centres are most crowded. With twenty-nine centres the caps force only two
within $2.05$ and fifteen within $2.1$, and the certificates nineteen and
twenty-two (\cref{fig:C-radial}).

Three limits remain. At $2.0161$, where the case of twenty-three close
centres of \cref{rem:what-c-needs} is decided, the single splits give
little: they prove eight centres within $2.0161$ at $M=29$, while the
optimum of the programme on the first case already exceeds the level when
that case asks for twelve there, for three at $M=25$ and $28$, and for one at
$M=26$ and $27$. Twenty-four centres lie within
$2.444$ by \cref{lem:twentyfour-close}; a single split brings that radius
down to $2.25$ at $M=30$, $2.3$ at $M=29$ and $2.35$ at $M=27$ and $28$,
but not below $2.4$ for $M=25$ or $26$, where the optimum on the first case
is $3.1124$ and $3.1068$. And, as for (G), the counts say
nothing about the directions: the case of twenty-four close centres needs
each of them within $0.093$ of a root system, and the case of twenty-three
a certificate that tells a packing from the fictitious configurations of
\cref{rem:what-c-needs}.

\begin{figure}[tb]
\centering
\includegraphics[width=\textwidth]{fig_tikz_cradial}
\caption{\Cref{prop:C-radial}: for $M$ centres within $\sqrt6$ of $c$ with
$T(Y)\le8$, the least number within $2.05$, $2.1$, $2.15$ and $2.2$ of $c$,
as the case certificates prove it (filled marks) and as the caps alone force
it (open marks). At $M=24$ the counts are those of \cref{prop:G-radial},
and at $M=30$ and $\rho=2.1$ that of \cref{thm:count30}.}
\label{fig:C-radial}
\end{figure}

Statement (G) can be proved in full along one family: the root system with
its centres pushed out along their own rays, by any amounts the region
$T\le8$ allows. The gain is \cref{lem:pure-push}; what has to be controlled
is the part of $V(Y)$ that the inversion hull cuts away, and it lies in
small pyramids at the vertices of the $24$-cell.

\begin{thm}[(G) along the push-outs]\label{thm:G-pushout}
Let $u_1,\dots,u_{24}$ be the normalised roots of a copy of $\Rt$, let $Y$
consist of the $24$ centres $(2+\delta_i)u_i$ with $\delta_i\ge0$, and
suppose that $T(Y)\le8$. Then every $\delta_i$ is below $0.1971$, and
\[
  \vol\bigl(V(Y)\cap K(Y)\bigr)\ \ge\ 8+\tfrac15\sum_i\delta_i .
\]
In particular (G) holds for $Y$, with equality only when every $\delta_i=0$.
\end{thm}

\begin{proof}
Put $\eta_i=\delta_i/2$ and $Q=\{x:\langle x,u_i\rangle\le1\ \forall i\}$, the
$24$-cell, so that $V(Y)=\{x:\langle x,u_i\rangle\le1+\eta_i\ \forall i\}$
and $K(Y)=\conv\{0,\,2u_i/(1+\eta_i)\}$.

\emph{The size of the push-outs.} Moving a centre out along its ray shrinks
its cap in $B(\sqrt{3/2})$, and with it the union of the caps, so $T$ does
not decrease in any $\delta_i$. Hence $T(Y)$ is at least the value of $T$ on
the root system with only the largest $\delta_i$ kept. On that ray
\[
  T(\delta)=\tfrac98\pi^2-23S(2)-S(2+\delta)+88\,\Pi(1,1,\tfrac12)
  +8\,\Pi(1,1+\tfrac\delta2,\tfrac12),
\]
since caps whose normals meet at $90^\circ$ or more do not meet in
$B(\sqrt{3/2})$, and in ball arithmetic, with \cref{lem:pair-closed},
$T(0.1971)=8.0000152\ldots>8$. Therefore every $\delta_i<0.1971$ and
$\eta_i<0.09855$.

\emph{The gain.} By \cref{lem:pure-push},
$\vol V(Y)\ge8+\frac13\sum_i\bigl(1-(1-\eta_i)^4\bigr)$.

\emph{The loss.} Let $\eta=\max_i\eta_i$ and $\lambda=(1+\eta)^2$, so
$\lambda-1<0.207$. The hull $K_0=\conv\{2u_i\}$ is the $24$-cell
$\{x:\langle x,\hat v\rangle\le\sqrt2\}$, $v$ running over the vertices of
$Q$, and its facet in the direction of $v$ is the octahedron with vertices
$2u$ for the six roots $u$ through $v$, centred at $v$. Since
$V(Y)\subseteq(1+\eta)Q$ and $K(Y)\supseteq(1+\eta)^{-1}K_0$, a point $x$ of
$V(Y)\setminus K(Y)$ has $(1+\eta)x\in\lambda Q$ and
$\langle(1+\eta)x,\hat v\rangle>\sqrt2$ for some vertex $v$. In coordinates
with $v=\sqrt2e_1$, the six facets of $Q$ through $v$ are
$x_1\pm x_j\le\sqrt2$, $j=2,3,4$, so $|x_j|<(\lambda-1)x_1<\frac13x_1$.
Let $\eta_v$ be the largest $\eta_u$ over the six roots $u$ through $v$. As
$x\in V(Y)$, $x_1\pm x_j\le\sqrt2(1+\eta_v)$. As $x\notin K(Y)$, $x$ is not
in the pyramid from $0$ over the octahedron with vertices $2u/(1+\eta_v)$,
which $K(Y)$ contains because $2u/(1+\eta_u)$ lies farther out on the same
ray; that octahedron lies in the hyperplane $x_1=\sqrt2/(1+\eta_v)$ and
contains every point of it with $|x_j|$ below a third of $\sqrt2/(1+\eta_v)$,
so the ray through $x$ meets it and $x_1>\sqrt2/(1+\eta_v)$. Hence $x$ lies in
the truncated cone
$\{\sqrt2/(1+\eta_v)<x_1,\ |x_j|\le\sqrt2(1+\eta_v)-x_1\}$, whose section at
$x_1=\sqrt2(1+\eta_v)-s$ is a cube of side $2s$; its volume is
$\int_0^{h_v}8s^3\,ds=2h_v^4$ with
$h_v=\sqrt2\bigl((1+\eta_v)-(1+\eta_v)^{-1}\bigr)\le2\sqrt2\,\eta_v$. Each
facet of $Q$ has six vertices, so
\[
  \vol\bigl(V(Y)\setminus K(Y)\bigr)\le\sum_v128\,\eta_v^4
  \le128\sum_v\sum_{u\ni v}\eta_u^4=768\sum_i\eta_i^4 .
\]

\emph{Together.} $\vol(V(Y)\cap K(Y))\ge8+\sum_ig(\eta_i)$ with
$g(\eta)=\frac13(1-(1-\eta)^4)-768\eta^4$. The derivative of
$g(\eta)/\eta=\frac13(4-6\eta+4\eta^2-\eta^3)-768\eta^3$ is at most
$-2+\frac83\eta<0$, and at $\eta=0.09855$ its value is $0.4137\ldots$; so
$g(\eta_i)\ge\frac25\eta_i=\frac15\delta_i$ for every $i$.
\end{proof}

\begin{cor}[Centres on the rays of a root system]\label{cor:rays}
If exactly $24$ centres other than $c$ lie within $\sqrt6$ of $c$, all on
the rays from $c$ through the normalised roots of a copy of $\Rt$, then
$\vol(V_c)\ge8$, with equality only when they are at distance $2$ and $V_c$
is the $24$-cell.
\end{cor}

\begin{proof}
If $T(Y)>8$, then $\vol(V_c)\ge T(Y)>8$ by \cref{lem:no-triple}. Otherwise
$\vol(V_c)\ge\vol(V(Y)\cap K(Y))\ge8+\frac15\sum_i\delta_i$ by
\cref{prop:inversion-hull,thm:G-pushout}, and equality forces every
$\delta_i=0$, twenty-four contacts, and the $24$-cell by
\cref{lem:24-no-shell}.
\end{proof}

The inequality is far from tight: along the ray of one centre the part of
$V(Y)$ outside the hull stays below $10^{-6}$ up to $\delta=0.197$, against
$768\eta^4\approx7\cdot10^{-2}$ there. What stops the argument at the
push-outs is the gain: \cref{lem:pure-push} needs the normals to be the
roots, and with the directions tilted the contact cell itself can fall to
$8$ (\cref{prop:hexagon-loop}), so a proof off this family has to take the
gain from the push-outs that the tilts force.

Off the push-outs, the pair terms still say how far out the centres of (G)
can be. The counts below are those the case certificates of the proof of
\cref{thm:count30} give when they are run with twenty-four centres in place
of thirty, as \cref{prop:C-radial} does for twenty-five to thirty.

\begin{prop}[Where the twenty-four centres lie]\label{prop:G-radial}
Let $Y$ be a packing set of exactly $24$ centres with $2\le|y|<\sqrt6$ and
$T(Y)\le8$. Then at most $13$ centres of $Y$ lie beyond $2.0161$, at most
four beyond $2.05$, at most two beyond $2.1$, and at most one beyond $2.15$.
\end{prop}

\begin{proof}
For a radius $\rho$ and a count $c$, take the tree of the proof of
\cref{thm:count30} with $M=24$ and the single split $N(\rho)\le c-1$ against
$N(\rho)\ge c$. For $(\rho,c)=(2.0161,11)$, $(2.05,20)$, $(2.1,22)$ and
$(2.15,23)$ the first case carries a certificate of degree $12$ in the angle
and $4$ in the distance, and the largest value it gives over the count
vectors of that case is at most $3.10018$, $3.09836$, $3.07883$ and $3.09251$
respectively, below $\frac98\pi^2-8=3.10330$. So
$T(Y)>8$ in the first case, and $N(\rho)\ge c$ whenever $T(Y)\le8$. Each
certificate is checked as there: positivity by an exact $LDL^{\mathsf T}$
factorisation, the inequality $K\le\Pi$ on the admissible pairs by a branch
and bound in ball arithmetic,\footnote{At the antipodal pairs, $u=-1$, the caps are disjoint and $\Pi$
vanishes, so the kernel must be nonpositive there. A kernel computed from too
few samples along $u=-1$ can be slightly positive at such pairs and then fails
the branch and bound, which is why the programme refines its samples along
that edge as well; for $2.0161$ and $2.05$ it also keeps a margin of
$3\cdot10^{-5}$ and $5\cdot10^{-5}$, rather than $2\cdot10^{-5}$, wherever
the caps are disjoint.} the bounds on the bins, and the largest value over
the count vectors by exact dynamic programming.
\end{proof}

The caps alone give much less. Since $\sum_yS(|y|)\ge U(Y)\ge\frac98\pi^2-8$
when $T(Y)\le8$, and $S$ decreases, $k$ centres can lie beyond $\rho$ only if
$(24-k)S(2)+kS(\rho)\ge\frac98\pi^2-8$. That allows $19$ centres beyond
$2.0161$, six beyond $2.05$, three beyond $2.1$ and two beyond $2.15$, and one
beyond every radius below $\sqrt6$ (\cref{fig:G-radial}); the pair terms
remove six, two, one and one. Beyond $2.05$ they cannot remove more at this
degree: with the next count, $21$ at $2.05$, $23$ at $2.1$, or $24$ at $2.2$
or $2.25$, the optimum of the same programme already lies above the level,
at $3.1151$, $3.1248$, $3.1422$ and $3.1306$, so certificates of this kind
cannot exclude a single centre far out. That is still far from the
neighbourhood of \cref{thm:near-contact}, where $\sum_i\delta_i\le4\cdot10^{-3}$,
and it does not constrain the directions.

\begin{figure}[tb]
\centering
\includegraphics[width=\textwidth]{fig_tikz_radial}
\caption{\Cref{prop:G-radial}. (a) For twenty-four centres with $T(Y)\le8$,
the number that can lie beyond a radius $\rho$: the cap budget alone (step
curve, open marks) against the counts that the case certificates prove
(filled marks), $13$ beyond $2.0161$, $4$ beyond $2.05$, $2$ beyond $2.1$ and
$1$ beyond $2.15$.
(b) For the split
$N(\rho)\le c-1$ against $N(\rho)\ge c$, the bound on the union of the caps
in the first case: proved by a certificate (filled marks) and, for the next
count, the optimum of the programme of the same degree (open marks), which
is not a bound, against the level $\frac98\pi^2-8$ that $T(Y)>8$ needs.}
\label{fig:G-radial}
\end{figure}

\begin{remark}[What (G) needs]\label{rem:what-g-needs-2}
Statement (G) is sharp at the root system and holds near it:
\cref{thm:near-contact} proves it whenever $\sum_i\delta_i\le4\cdot10^{-3}$
and $d(W)\le\frac1{48}$, and \cref{thm:G-pushout} proves it for every
push-out of the root system in the region, whatever its size. The region
$T(Y)\le8$ in which it is asked is larger than the first, but not by much. Along the root system pushed out evenly the pair
terms reach $8$ at $\sum_i\delta_i=0.155$, and along the ray of one centre
at $\delta=0.197$; every local minimum of $T$ that a descent from random
starts reaches with $24$ centres is the root system itself; and
configurations far from it, such as the packing with
$\vol(V(Y)\cap K(Y))=4.750$ of \cref{rem:what-g-needs}, have $T$ far above $8$.
Inside the region the structure of the volume is known to second order. The
push-outs raise it at the rate $\frac23$ (\cref{thm:near-contact}), and on
the first-order packing cone the Hessian loses at most
$(\sum_i\delta_i)^2$ (\cref{prop:cone-min}), so that the second-order model
$\frac23S-\frac12S^2$ is positive up to $S=\frac43$, far beyond $0.197$. The
tilts alone never lower the volume at second order (\cref{prop:tilt-block}),
and along the closed curves of \cref{prop:hexagon-loop} they leave it at
exactly $8$; but those curves break the packing constraint at first order,
the largest inner product rising to $4C^2/(4C^2+3)>\frac12$, so a packing can
follow them only by moving centres out, which the first-order term rewards
at the rate $\frac23$. A proof of (G) has to capture this mechanism; the
searches of \cref{rem:what-g-needs} find no configuration below $8$.

A proof needs two quantitative statements. The first is a localisation: if
$|Y|=24$ and $T(Y)\le8$, then $Y$ lies within an explicit distance of a copy
of $\sqrt2\,\Rt$. For contacts this is the uniqueness theorem of
\cite{LLM24}; its robust form is proved only at slack $2\cdot10^{-26}$
(\cref{thm:m24-robust}), and \cref{prop:second-code} shows that no form of it
that looks at the directions alone can pass slack $0.017$, so the
localisation has to use the distances, through $T$ itself.
\Cref{prop:G-radial} is the part of it that the pair terms give: it bounds
how many centres lie far from $c$, but not where their directions point. The second is the
volume bound in that neighbourhood, which needs the third-order remainder
and the part of $V(Y)$ outside the inversion hull under control
(\cref{rem:second-order-gap}); \cref{thm:near-contact} provides it up to
$\sum_i\delta_i=4\cdot10^{-3}$, a factor of about forty short of the region.
Together, (G) and the remaining counts of (C) would prove
\cref{conj:shell} and with it the bound $\pi^2/16$ for the density of every
packing of $\mathbb{R}^4$, which is open; the kissing problem in dimension
four \cite{Mus08} and the uniqueness of its solution \cite{LLM24} each needed
a new semidefinite method, and the computations of this section indicate
that the remaining step does too.
\end{remark}


\section{Comparison with other strategies}\label{sec:comparison-methods}

\subsection{Local against global}

The Cohn--Elkies method \cite{CE03}, and its sharpened form in dimensions
$8$ and $24$ \cite{Via17,CKM17}, is global and Fourier-analytic: one
auxiliary function certifies optimality over all configurations at once.
The argument here is local. It bounds one Voronoi cell at a time, and it
reduces the bound for a cell either to an extremal problem for one fixed
polytope (\cref{sec:cap-theorem}) or to an inequality between the distances
and pair angles of the neighbours inside a ball in which no three caps meet,
which a semidefinite kernel certifies
(\cref{sec:strict-inequality,sec:closure}). In dimension four the global
route is closed: by the result of Li \cite{Li25} recalled in
\cref{sec:history-new}, no auxiliary function brings the linear programming
bound down to $\pi^2/16$, and the three-point refinement of
\cite{CdLS22} stays above it as well. A local method is therefore not a
second choice here.

\subsection{What each method asks of the reader}

A linear or semidefinite programming bound certifies positivity of a
high-dimensional object found by a numerical solver. Such certificates can
be converted to exact rational form \cite{Par03,Las01,CdLL24}, and the ones
used here are: every certificate of this paper is a finite list of rational
matrices whose positivity is checked by an exact factorisation, together with
an inequality on a compact domain checked by branch and bound in interval
arithmetic (\cref{sec:reproducibility-new}). The magic-function constructions
are closed form once written down, but checking that a given function has the
stated decay, sign pattern and Fourier eigenvalue takes complex-analytic and
modular machinery. The formal proof of the Kepler conjecture \cite{Hal17}
lies at the far end: every step is machine-checked. The arguments of
\cref{sec:root-system,sec:cap-theorem} need no solver at all, and the finite
parts of the others are checked in Lean (\cref{sec:formal-part}).

\subsection{Other dimensions}\label{sec:other-dims-new}

The lattices $D_5$, $E_6$ and $E_7$, with their Voronoi cells
\cite{CS1988,Cox73}, are the natural next cases for the same argument.
The truncation to a ball in which no three caps meet, and the labelled
kernels that bound the union of the caps, carry over in form to every
dimension. The cap argument of \cref{sec:cap-theorem} needs a fixed polytope
containing the relevant body, an enclosure that is tight in the critical
direction, and a cap formula for the enclosure; whether these exist for
$E_6$ or $E_7$ is not investigated here.

\section{Conclusion}\label{sec:status}

\subsection{What is proved}

The twenty-four-cell conjecture holds at every centre whose neighbours
within $2\sqrt2$ all touch it (\cref{thm:local-general}). The proof divides
by the number of contacts: at most $22$ by the covering estimate
(\cref{cor:m22}), $23$ by the pair-angle inequality and its three-point
certificate (\cref{thm:m23,thm:certificate}), and $24$ by the classification
of twenty-four-point kissing configurations (\cref{thm:m24}), proved here
from the positive kernel of \cite{LLM24}, every property of which that the
proof uses is verified (\cref{prop:verified}). With one deviating direction
the bound has an elementary proof, through the extremal cap of a
cross-polytope (\cref{thm:local-uncond,thm:cap-inequality}).

The conjecture holds also at every centre with at most twenty-three other
centres within $\sqrt6$ (\cref{thm:m23-labelled}), with at least thirty-one
(\cref{thm:count31}), or with thirty outside one configuration type
(\cref{thm:count30}); at every centre whose twenty-four neighbours within
$\sqrt6$ lie on the rays of a root system (\cref{cor:rays}), whose contacts
contain enough of a root system (\cref{cor:root-subsets}), or whose
neighbours within $\sqrt6$ are within $\varepsilon_0=4\cdot10^{-26}$ of
contact (\cref{thm:near-contact}); and, by \cref{thm:main-general}, every
periodic or saturated packing whose centres are all of these kinds has
density at most $\pi^2/16$. Along the way the paper proves that the kissing
number of $\mathbb{R}^4$ is stable under a slack of $0.008$
(\cref{thm:kissing-stable}), that twenty-four centres near a root system
leave no room for another within $\sqrt6$ (\cref{cor:no-room}), that the
root system and the deletions of one root are infinitesimally rigid
(\cref{prop:deletion-rigid,cor:root-rigid}), that a saturated configuration
of twenty-three contacts shares at most twenty directions with any copy of
$\Rt$ (\cref{cor:meet20}), and it computes the second order of the cell
volume at the root system exactly (\cref{sec:second-order}).

\subsection{What is open}

What is not proved is \cref{conj:shell}: a centre with between $24$ and $30$
other centres within $\sqrt6$, one of them at a distance between
$2+\varepsilon_0$ and $\sqrt6$. By \cref{prop:reduction-G} it follows from
statement (G) for twenty-four centres and statement (C) for twenty-five to
thirty, and together these would give $\Delta_4=\pi^2/16$
(\cref{cor:conj-density}). Statement (C) is proved from thirty-one centres
on, and at thirty it leaves only twenty-four centres within $2.25$ and six
beyond $2.4$ (\cref{rem:thirty-left}). Statement (G) is proved along the
push-outs of the root system and near it
(\cref{thm:G-pushout,thm:near-contact}), and its second order is
nonnegative on the packing cone (\cref{prop:cone-min,prop:tilt-block}).

Both statements need a localisation that the methods of this paper do not
supply: that twenty-four centres with $T\le8$ lie near a copy of
$\sqrt2\,\Rt$. A localisation through the directions alone cannot pass slack
$0.01698$, where a second twenty-four-point code appears
(\cref{prop:second-code}); a margin on pairs at the second level of the
hierarchy gives out at slack about $5\cdot10^{-4}$, because the root system
itself uses it up (\cref{prop:margin-floor}); and kernels on pairs, even
labelled by distance, do not exclude twenty-five centres at any degree
tried (\cref{rem:kernel-limit}). The localisation therefore has to use the
distances through $T$, at the level of triples or higher.

\subsection{Outlook}

Two routes are visible. The first is a labelled certificate at the second
level of the hierarchy, a kernel on subsets of at most two centres that
carries their distances from $c$, for the statement that twenty-four centres
with $T\le8$ lie near a root system; combined with the volume bound near
the root system, which \cref{thm:near-contact} provides in a smaller
neighbourhood and \cref{sec:second-order} describes to second order, it
would prove (G) and, through \cref{cor:no-room}, the case of twenty-four
close centres in (C). The unlabelled version of that programme, which
settles twenty-four contacts, is already large \cite{LLM24}, and the
labelled one is larger. The second route is the residual case at thirty
alone, where a kernel that adds to the labelled two-point kernel a
three-point kernel typed by distance classes brings the optimum of the
programme on its sample points below the level $\frac98\pi^2-8$, but not by
a margin that survives the passage from samples to the whole domain; a finer
sampling or a higher degree may close it, and with it (C) at thirty.

A third route avoids (G) and (C) as statements about single cells.
\Cref{lem:redistribution} needs only a transfer of volume between
neighbouring centres, antisymmetric and bounded, after which every centre
holds at least $8$. This is the shape of Hales's proof of the Kepler
conjecture, where the score of a decomposition star replaces the volume of
a Voronoi cell \cite{Hal05}. In dimension four the transfer can vanish at
every centre covered by \cref{thm:local-general,thm:intro-closure}; what
has to be shown is that at a centre of \cref{conj:shell}, with $24$ to
$30$ neighbours within $\sqrt6$ and one of them not in contact, the
neighbours whose cells exceed $8$ can supply the deficit. Whether a
transfer of this kind exists is open, and it is a weaker statement than
the twenty-four-cell conjecture, since it can hold even if some cell has
volume below $8$.

\section{Computer verification}\label{sec:reproducibility-new}

Every computation on which a proof in this paper depends is of one of two
kinds, and the kind is named where the computation is used. The first is
exact arithmetic, in $\mathbb{Q}$, $\mathbb{Q}(\sqrt2)$, $\mathbb{Q}(\sqrt3)$
or $\mathbb{Z}[\phi]$, with no rounding at any step. It covers the vertex
enumeration of $Q$ and the box certificate of \cref{sec:cap-theorem}, the
rigidity and root-meet arguments of \cref{sec:m23-explicit,sec:root-meet},
the enumeration of \cref{prop:cell600}, the identities of
\cref{prop:hexagon-loop,prop:second-code}, the positivity of every
certificate matrix by an exact $LDL^{\mathsf T}$ factorisation
\cite[\S4.1]{GVL13}, and the value of every certified bound. The second is
interval or ball arithmetic with outward rounding \cite[Ch.~5]{MKC09},
\cite{Joh17}: every computed quantity is an interval that contains the true
value, and a sign is decided only when the interval excludes zero. It covers
the inequalities that the certificates of
\cref{thm:certificate,thm:m23-labelled,thm:kissing-stable,thm:count31,thm:count30,prop:C-radial,prop:G-radial}
must satisfy on their domains, which are decided by branch and bound, and
the constants of \cref{thm:near-contact,lem:twentyfour-close,cor:no-room,thm:G-pushout}.

Floating-point arithmetic enters in two places only. It is used to find
certificates, which are then rounded to rationals and checked as above, so
that a solver's tolerance never reaches a proof. And it produces the values
quoted to show where a method stops, such as the optima of semidefinite
programmes in \cref{sec:remains,sec:G-and-C}; these are marked where they
occur, and no proof uses them.

\subsection{Independent arithmetic}

The certificate of \cite{LLM24} is verified from the deposited data
\cite{LLM24data} in code that shares nothing with its authors', with the
exact rational arithmetic of FLINT and the ball arithmetic of Arb
\cite{Joh17}. The semidefinite programmes behind
\cref{thm:certificate,thm:kissing-stable,thm:count31,thm:count30} are posed
with \cite{DB16} and solved by the interior-point method of \cite{GC26};
what enters a proof is the rounding of the solution to rationals, checked
as above. The box certificates are regenerated by the rule of
\cref{app:box-cover-algo}, so that every quoted box count is exact and
reproducible. The second-level programmes of \cref{sec:remains} are
solved by the solver of \cite{LdL24} at a working precision of $128$ bits;
no certificate is extracted from them, and their values are quoted only to
show where that method stops.

\subsection{The formal part}\label{sec:formal-part}

Seventeen parts of the paper are also verified in the Lean~4 proof
assistant \cite{MU21}, with no external library; each is a finite
computation or a polynomial identity, and \cref{tab:formal} lists them.
Identities are proved over every commutative ring by Lean's ring
normaliser, so that they hold verbatim over $\mathbb{R}$. Finite
computations are settled either by the kernel itself or by compiled
evaluation; the second trusts the compiler as well as the kernel, and the
axiom report of each theorem says which was used. No proof in the formal
part is left incomplete. What is formalised is the arithmetic. That a
certificate turns a programme's inequality into a theorem, that a form is
the Hessian of the volume, or that a cap has the stated closed form is
proved in the text and not formalised, because the geometry of polytopes
and cap measures in $\mathbb{R}^4$ has no counterpart in the libraries on
which a formalisation could be built.

\subsection{What is printed and what is archived}

Every exact claim that a proof uses is stated in the text: as a displayed
fraction or polynomial, as a table entry, or as the rule that produces it.
The cap inequality of \cref{sec:cap-theorem} is self-contained in this
sense, since the cap formula, the monotonicity, the three polynomial
inequalities and the subdivision rule are given in full and the rule
regenerates the $303$ boxes. The objects that are not printed are the
matrices of the semidefinite certificates and the lists of boxes of the
branch and bounds; they are in the archive described below, together with
the programmes that check them and the Lean development.

\section*{Data and code availability}

The supplementary package, with the certificates, the programmes that
check them, the logs of every run and the Lean development of
\cref{sec:formal-part}, is archived on Zenodo
\cite{D4Zenodo}. The certificate verified in \cref{sec:certificate-checked}
is the data set of \cite{LLM24data}, deposited by its authors under the MIT
licence; it is included, with its checksum and licence notice, so that the
proof of \cref{thm:twentyfour-points} can be rerun from the archive alone.
Part of the code of the package, including the re-verification of the
computations, the programmes behind
\cref{sec:noncontact,sec:closure,sec:second-order,sec:m24-robust,sec:G-and-C}
and part of the Lean development, was developed with the assistance of
Claude, an AI model made by Anthropic, and its computations were run with
it.

\section{Methods of computation}\label{app:code-index}

This appendix states the two procedures that the certificates of the paper
share, and then how each certificate is found and checked.

\subsection*{The box-covering certificate}\label{app:box-cover-algo}

Let $R$ be a box, let $F$ be a function on $R$ whose sign is to be
certified, and let $\beta(R')$ be, for every sub-box $R'$, an
outward-rounded enclosure of the range of $F$ on $R'$ in the sense of
\cite[Ch.~5]{MKC09}. The certificate is produced by the following
procedure. A list of boxes starts as $\{R\}$. A box $R'$ is removed from
the list; if $\beta(R')$ has the required sign, $R'$ is recorded as
certified; otherwise, if the longest side of $R'$ is below a tolerance
$\varepsilon$ fixed before the run, the procedure stops and reports
failure; otherwise $R'$ is bisected along its longest side and both halves
are put on the list. The procedure ends when the list is empty.

A successful run returns finitely many boxes whose union is $R$, with
disjoint interiors, and this is checked on the box boundaries in exact
rational arithmetic rather than assumed from the construction; so every
quoted box count is exact. There is one way to fail, a box of width below
$\varepsilon$ on which the sign is still undecided, and then no partial
certificate is reported. In \cref{thm:cap-inequality} the enclosure is the
corner value supplied by \cref{lem:monotone}; in
\cref{thm:certificate,thm:m23-labelled,thm:kissing-stable} it is a
second-order Taylor form with rigorous tables of $\omega$, $\omega'$ and
$\omega''$; in \cref{thm:count31,thm:count30} it is the largest Bernstein
coefficient \cite{Far12} of the kernel on the box less the corner value of
$\Pi$ in ball arithmetic.

\subsection*{Rounding a semidefinite solution}

A semidefinite programme is solved in floating point with its inequalities
imposed at finitely many sample points. After each solution the inequality
is tested at a much larger set of further points, weighted towards the
edges and corners of the domain, and the worst are added to the sample
until none is violated. The matrices are then rounded to dyadic rationals,
and from that point on everything is exact: positivity by $LDL^{\mathsf T}$,
the value of the bound, and the inequality on the whole domain by the
procedure above. A margin is kept in the sampled inequality, so that the
rounded kernel still satisfies it between the sample points.

\subsection*{The certificates}

\emph{The cap inequality.} The chain of \cref{sec:cap-theorem} is exact from
end to end: the vertex enumeration of $Q$, with $25$ vertices and
$\vol Q=\frac{25}3$, \cref{lem:crosspolytope}, the monotonicity of
\cref{lem:monotone}, the three polynomial inequalities of the case $|S|=1$,
and the $303$ boxes of the case $|S|\ge2$, whose largest corner value is
$0.99755\ldots$.

\emph{The pair inequality.} The polynomial $P$ of \cref{thm:certificate} is
expanded exactly from the certificate entries, multiplied by $315$ so that
every coefficient is an integer over a power of two, and bounded on the
ordered admissible domain over $421\,881$ boxes. The labelled certificate
of \cref{thm:m23-labelled} is checked by the same procedure on the regions
of its proof.

\emph{The cited certificate.} The verification of \cref{prop:verified}
reads the deposited data \cite{LLM24data}, checks their format and the
positive definiteness of the $127$ blocks, rebuilds the zonal matrices
without holding the expansion of the integrand at once, checks the four
polynomial identities and the value $24$ of the bound, and isolates the
zeros of $\sigma_2$ by a Sturm sequence.

\emph{The cardinality certificates.} The certificates of
\cref{thm:kissing-stable} are found by a sampled programme, rounded, and
checked: positivity by $LDL^{\mathsf T}$, the bound, and both branch and
bounds of the three-point condition in outward-rounded interval
arithmetic.

\emph{The second order at the root system.} The form $H$ of
\cref{prop:hessian-d4} is assembled from the integral model of the
$24$-cell over the triangles of the tight pairs; the certificate of
\cref{prop:cone-min} is the dual of a Shor relaxation, averaged over the
symmetry group, made exact, and checked by exact $LDL^{\mathsf T}$.

\emph{The explicit neighbourhood.} The constant $\varepsilon_0$ of
\cref{thm:near-contact} is computed from the deposited sums of squares in
ball arithmetic, and the bracket $\beta$ of its proof in exact rational
arithmetic on a grid of step $10^{-5}$.

\emph{The counts.} The kernel of \cref{thm:count31} is found as above, the
inequality being tested after each solution at two million further
admissible triples, weighted towards the edges $|y|=2$ and $|y'|=\sqrt6$ and
the boundary $u=a(|y|,|y'|)$. The Bernstein subdivision of the kernel has
its floating-point error bounded a priori: each midpoint step is an average,
so it adds at most one unit in the last place of the largest coefficient.
The certificates of \cref{thm:count30} are found and checked in the same
way, one for each case of the tree, with a margin in the pair inequality
tried at $2\cdot10^{-5}$ and lowered until the bound of the case stays below
$\frac98\pi^2-8$ by $2\cdot10^{-4}$. The tree is checked from its splits
alone: each child adds one bound on one $N(\rho)$, and its other bound on
$N(\rho)$ is no tighter than what the bounds of the parent, together with
$N(2.0161)\le24$ and $N(d_{\max})=30$, imply. The largest value of
$\sum_bn_b(m_b+sS(e_b))$ over the count vectors of a case is found by
dynamic programming over the bins in exact rational arithmetic, with the
values $S(e_b)$ rounded up from ball arithmetic. The single splits of
\cref{prop:C-radial,prop:G-radial} are found and checked in the same way.

\subsection*{Software}

The exact and ball arithmetic is that of FLINT and Arb \cite{Joh17}, with
\cite{Meu17} on a second, independent path for the exact rational
computations; convex hulls are computed by \cite{BDH96}, and the numerical
linear algebra uses \cite{Har20,Vir20}. The formal part uses Lean~4
\cite{MU21}.
